### Tinker: Diffusion's Gift to 3D--Multi-View Consistent Editing From Sparse Inputs without Per-Scene Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14811v1)
- **Authors**: Canyu Zhao, Xiaoman Li, Tianjian Feng, Zhiyue Zhao, Hao Chen, Chunhua Shen
- **Abstract**: We introduce Tinker, a versatile framework for high-fidelity 3D editing that operates in both one-shot and few-shot regimes without any per-scene finetuning. Unlike prior techniques that demand extensive per-scene optimization to ensure multi-view consistency or to produce dozens of consistent edited input views, Tinker delivers robust, multi-view consistent edits from as few as one or two images. This capability stems from repurposing pretrained diffusion models, which unlocks their latent 3D awareness. To drive research in this space, we curate the first large-scale multi-view editing dataset and data pipeline, spanning diverse scenes and styles. Building on this dataset, we develop our framework capable of generating multi-view consistent edited views without per-scene training, which consists of two novel components: (1) Referring multi-view editor: Enables precise, reference-driven edits that remain coherent across all viewpoints. (2) Any-view-to-video synthesizer: Leverages spatial-temporal priors from video diffusion to perform high-quality scene completion and novel-view generation even from sparse inputs. Through extensive experiments, Tinker significantly reduces the barrier to generalizable 3D content creation, achieving state-of-the-art performance on editing, novel-view synthesis, and rendering enhancement tasks. We believe that Tinker represents a key step towards truly scalable, zero-shot 3D editing. Project webpage: https://aim-uofa.github.io/Tinker
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TINKER, a novel framework for high-fidelity 3D editing.  Unlike existing methods that require extensive per-scene optimization or numerous consistent edited input views, TINKER delivers robust, multi-view consistent edits from only one or two images by leveraging pre-trained diffusion models.  The key contributions are: (1) a referring multi-view editor that enables precise, reference-driven edits maintaining cross-view coherence, and (2) an any-view-to-video synthesizer that uses video diffusion priors for scene completion and novel-view generation even from sparse inputs.  To facilitate research, the authors create a large-scale multi-view editing dataset and a corresponding data pipeline.  Experiments demonstrate TINKER's ability to perform editing, novel-view synthesis, and rendering enhancement, achieving state-of-the-art performance without per-scene training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty is moderate to high. The core idea of directly leveraging the latent 3D awareness of pre-trained diffusion models for multi-view consistent editing is innovative.  While some prior work has used diffusion models for 3D editing, they typically involve fine-tuning on a per-scene basis or generating numerous consistent 2D views. TINKER's ability to achieve similar results with a *single* or *few* input views and *no per-scene optimization* is a significant advance. The introduced pipeline for generating referring-based editing datasets and the rethinking of editing as a reconstruction problem for the sparse-view completion model are also noteworthy novel components.

*   **Significance:** The significance is high. Removing the need for per-scene optimization substantially reduces the barrier to entry for 3D editing, making it more accessible and scalable.  The new dataset should foster further research in generalizable 3D editing. The ability to enhance rendering quality and potentially compress video data through depth-aware reconstruction adds practical value. The approach is well positioned to take advantage of future improvements in large-scale diffusion models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Generalizability:**  A key strength is the framework's generalizability. The approach is not tied to specific scenes and can be applied to various styles and editing tasks.
    *   **Efficiency:** The elimination of per-scene fine-tuning leads to significant computational efficiency.
    *   **Multi-view consistency:**  The approach effectively addresses the challenge of maintaining multi-view consistency, a critical aspect of 3D editing.
    *   **High-quality results:** The experiments showcase the capability of the method for generating high-quality results, with better quality than previous methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper highlights the improvement over per-scene fine-tuning, the *scale* and nature of the fine-tuning on the *multi-view consistency editing dataset* is a component of the approach and not wholly eliminated. A deeper discussion about the size, characteristics, and limitations of this pre-processing phase would be beneficial.
    *   The paper mentions limitations regarding edits involving large geometric deformations, but a more detailed analysis of the types of scenes and edits where TINKER performs poorly would improve its understanding. The limitations section is rather broad.
    *   The lack of a user study or application analysis to assess the user experience of TINKER is missing.

*   **Potential Influence:**  TINKER has the potential to significantly influence the field of 3D content creation. It offers a more practical and scalable approach to 3D editing, which could accelerate the adoption of 3D technologies in various applications.  The introduced data generation pipeline and the multi-view dataset should encourage future research in this area.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant advancement in 3D editing. The innovative approach of leveraging pre-trained diffusion models and the introduction of a new dataset address key limitations of existing methods. The framework's generalizability, efficiency, and ability to maintain multi-view consistency are compelling strengths. While the limitations regarding geometric deformations and the lack of a user study prevent a higher score, TINKER makes a notable contribution that could shape the future of 3D content creation. The removal of per-scene optimization is significant and the results are impressive. Therefore, the approach is close to excellence but there is room for greater detail and additional experimentation.

- **Classification**: cs.CV

### TransLight: Image-Guided Customized Lighting Control with Generative Decoupling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14814v1)
- **Authors**: Zongming Li, Lianghui Zhu, Haocheng Shen, Longjin Ran, Wenyu Liu, Xinggang Wang
- **Abstract**: Most existing illumination-editing approaches fail to simultaneously provide customized control of light effects and preserve content integrity. This makes them less effective for practical lighting stylization requirements, especially in the challenging task of transferring complex light effects from a reference image to a user-specified target image. To address this problem, we propose TransLight, a novel framework that enables high-fidelity and high-freedom transfer of light effects. Extracting the light effect from the reference image is the most critical and challenging step in our method. The difficulty lies in the complex geometric structure features embedded in light effects that are highly coupled with content in real-world scenarios. To achieve this, we first present Generative Decoupling, where two fine-tuned diffusion models are used to accurately separate image content and light effects, generating a newly curated, million-scale dataset of image-content-light triplets. Then, we employ IC-Light as the generative model and train our model with our triplets, injecting the reference lighting image as an additional conditioning signal. The resulting TransLight model enables customized and natural transfer of diverse light effects. Notably, by thoroughly disentangling light effects from reference images, our generative decoupling strategy endows TransLight with highly flexible illumination control. Experimental results establish TransLight as the first method to successfully transfer light effects across disparate images, delivering more customized illumination control than existing techniques and charting new directions for research in illumination harmonization and editing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TransLight: Image-Guided Customized Lighting Control with Generative Decoupling":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes TransLight, a novel framework for transferring light effects from a reference image to a target image with customized control over the lighting. The core of the approach is a "Generative Decoupling" technique that uses two fine-tuned diffusion models to separate image content and light effects, resulting in image-content-light triplets.  This allows for flexible adjustment of the position, direction, and intensity of the transferred light effect. The framework employs IC-Light as the base generative model and trains it using the generated triplets. Experimental results are presented to demonstrate the method's ability to transfer light effects across diverse images and its superiority to existing illumination editing techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the Generative Decoupling strategy and the application of this to light effect transfer. While style transfer has been explored extensively, light *effect* transfer, with the added constraint of geometric control, is a relatively unexplored area. The use of diffusion models for decoupling content and light is a reasonable application of existing technology, but the execution, dataset construction and evaluation are novel. The combination of a light removal and a light extraction model for this purpose seems effective and introduces a new way of thinking in the image editing field.

*   **Significance:**  The significance stems from the practical applicability of the approach.  Customized lighting control is a valuable capability for image editing and stylization, with potential applications in photography, virtual content creation, and other areas. The ability to disentangle light effects and transfer them with geometric control addresses a current limitation in existing illumination editing methods. The ability to transfer lighting effects from one image to another in such a controllable and relatively seamless way has implications for creative workflows.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The Generative Decoupling approach is well-motivated and seems to work effectively.
    *   The construction of a large-scale dataset of image-content-light triplets is a significant undertaking and a valuable contribution.
    *   The framework allows for flexible control over the transferred light effects (position, direction, intensity).
    *   The experimental results show that the method outperforms existing illumination editing techniques, IC-Light. The inclusion of qualitative comparisons with SeedEdit and GPT-Image is valuable, despite the closed-source nature.
    *   Ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of the different components of the framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The generative decoupling part relies on a fixed prompt and a limited domain in generating the light images. This may affect the model's ability to extract very unusual or complex light effects.
    *   The quantitative evaluation relies heavily on similarity metrics (PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS) and Light FID, which may not fully capture the perceptual quality of the transferred light effects. More thorough user studies might be beneficial.
    *   The inference stage depends on the performance of the light extraction model, and limitations in the extraction process can impact the final results.
    *   While the paper presents qualitative results on challenging scenarios, the range of demonstrated light effects is somewhat limited (lens flares, shadows, sun rays). More examples showcasing transfer across different light *types* would strengthen the evaluation.
    *   The implementation details are not discussed at length, which might limit reproducibility.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in image editing, particularly in areas related to illumination manipulation and style transfer. The Generative Decoupling approach could be adapted for other image editing tasks, and the created dataset could serve as a valuable resource for the community. The introduction of this novel light effects transfer task could open up new avenues for research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to image editing, particularly in the area of illumination control. The Generative Decoupling strategy is well-motivated and effectively executed, and the experimental results demonstrate the method's superiority to existing techniques. However, the reliance on similarity metrics for evaluation, the limitations in extraction and generation, and the limited implementation details do temper the overall impact. The method makes a solid and well-justified contribution, but there are some areas to improve for greater impact.

### Evaluating Retrieval-Augmented Generation vs. Long-Context Input for Clinical Reasoning over EHRs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14817v1)
- **Authors**: Skatje Myers, Dmitriy Dligach, Timothy A. Miller, Samantha Barr, Yanjun Gao, Matthew Churpek, Anoop Mayampurath, Majid Afshar
- **Abstract**: Electronic health records (EHRs) are long, noisy, and often redundant, posing a major challenge for the clinicians who must navigate them. Large language models (LLMs) offer a promising solution for extracting and reasoning over this unstructured text, but the length of clinical notes often exceeds even state-of-the-art models' extended context windows. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) offers an alternative by retrieving task-relevant passages from across the entire EHR, potentially reducing the amount of required input tokens. In this work, we propose three clinical tasks designed to be replicable across health systems with minimal effort: 1) extracting imaging procedures, 2) generating timelines of antibiotic use, and 3) identifying key diagnoses. Using EHRs from actual hospitalized patients, we test three state-of-the-art LLMs with varying amounts of provided context, using either targeted text retrieval or the most recent clinical notes. We find that RAG closely matches or exceeds the performance of using recent notes, and approaches the performance of using the models' full context while requiring drastically fewer input tokens. Our results suggest that RAG remains a competitive and efficient approach even as newer models become capable of handling increasingly longer amounts of text.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper evaluates the effectiveness of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) versus long-context language models (LLMs) for clinical reasoning tasks using electronic health records (EHRs).  It introduces three clinical tasks: extracting imaging procedures, generating antibiotic timelines, and identifying key diagnoses, designed to be replicable across different healthcare systems. The study compares RAG, using recent clinical notes, and providing the full context window to three state-of-the-art LLMs.  The results suggest that RAG closely matches or exceeds the performance of using recent notes, and often approaches the performance of using the models' full context window, while significantly reducing the number of input tokens. The authors conclude that RAG remains a competitive and efficient approach, even as LLMs become capable of handling longer text sequences.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in clinical NLP: how to effectively utilize LLMs with lengthy and noisy EHR data. The focus on real-world EHR data, rather than solely relying on curated datasets like MIMIC, increases the practical value of the findings.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The design of the three clinical tasks aims for reproducibility across different health systems, which is a major strength, addressing a known limitation of existing clinical NLP benchmarks.
    *   **Efficiency Focus:** The emphasis on token efficiency is highly relevant, considering the computational costs associated with processing long text sequences with LLMs. The comparative analysis of RAG versus full-context approaches provides valuable insights into trade-offs between accuracy and efficiency.
    *   **Clear Experimental Setup:** The methodology is well-defined, including the chunking strategy, embedding model, and evaluation metrics. This transparency allows for replication and further investigation.
    *   **Interesting finding:** A key takeaway is the fact that despite the increasing availability of longer context models, RAGs potential is still very viable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Task Specificity:** While the three tasks are clinically relevant, they might not fully represent the spectrum of clinical reasoning tasks. The generalizability of the findings to more complex clinical decision-making scenarios could be limited.
    *   **Retrieval Strategy Simplification:** The study uses a relatively simple retrieval strategy based on cosine similarity between a fixed query and text chunks. More sophisticated retrieval methods, such as those incorporating knowledge graphs or hierarchical retrieval, could potentially improve RAG performance further, but they were not explored.
    *   **Evaluation Limitations:**  Although automated evaluation is used, the reliance on ICD-10 code matching for diagnosis generation might oversimplify the assessment of clinical reasoning.  The study acknowledges some limitations in the automatic evaluation, especially for subjective tasks like diagnosis generation. It states "less-than-perfect performance of this model may have introduced some noise to this evaluation.".
    *   **Lack of Ablation Studies:**  The study could be strengthened by including ablation experiments to identify the contribution of key design choices (e.g., the choice of embedding model, the chunk size, and retrieval query) and assessing whether the performance is robust to changes in them.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper builds on existing research on RAG for long documents.
    *   The adaptation and evaluation of RAG for clinical reasoning over real-world EHR data are valuable.
    *   The focus on reproducibility across different health systems is a significant contribution.
    *   The systematic comparison of RAG with full-context LLMs highlights the benefits of RAG in terms of token efficiency.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by providing a practical evaluation of RAG for clinical reasoning using real-world EHR data. The focus on token efficiency and reproducibility is particularly valuable. However, the limitations in task complexity, retrieval strategy, and evaluation methods slightly temper the overall impact. It is also somewhat incremental given prior work showing the utility of RAG, and long-context LLMs for a variety of different tasks. I assign a score of 6 to reflect this mix of novelty, practical value, and limitations.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Long Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Across Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14828v1)
- **Authors**: Josh Barua, Seun Eisape, Kayo Yin, Alane Suhr
- **Abstract**: Scaling inference through long chains-of-thought (CoTs) has unlocked impressive reasoning capabilities in large language models (LLMs), yet the reasoning process remains almost exclusively English-centric. We construct translated versions of two popular English reasoning datasets, fine-tune Qwen 2.5 (7B) and Qwen 3 (8B) models, and present a systematic study of long CoT generation across French, Japanese, Latvian, and Swahili. Our experiments reveal three key findings. First, the efficacy of using English as a pivot language varies by language: it provides no benefit for French, improves performance when used as the reasoning language for Japanese and Latvian, and proves insufficient for Swahili where both task comprehension and reasoning remain poor. Second, extensive multilingual pretraining in Qwen 3 narrows but does not eliminate the cross-lingual performance gap. A lightweight fine-tune using only 1k traces still improves performance by over 30\% in Swahili. Third, data quality versus scale trade-offs are language dependent: small, carefully curated datasets suffice for English and French, whereas larger but noisier corpora prove more effective for Swahili and Latvian. Together, these results clarify when and why long CoTs transfer across languages and provide translated datasets to foster equitable multilingual reasoning research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning across multiple languages. The authors translate English reasoning datasets into French, Japanese, Latvian, and Swahili, then fine-tune Qwen models (2.5-7B and 3-8B) on these datasets. The study investigates the effectiveness of using English as a pivot language, the impact of multilingual pretraining, and the data quality vs. scale trade-offs in different languages. The findings suggest that: (1) English as a pivot language isn't universally effective and its benefit varies across languages. (2) Extensive multilingual pretraining reduces, but doesn't eliminate, the cross-lingual performance gap, with targeted fine-tuning still providing significant gains. (3) The optimal data quality/quantity trade-off for fine-tuning is language-dependent. The authors release their translated datasets to encourage further research in equitable multilingual reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable empirical analysis of CoT reasoning across multiple languages, a relatively underexplored area. The explicit examination of factors like the pivot language's effectiveness, the benefits of large-scale multilingual pretraining, and the data quality/quantity trade-offs in diverse languages is a worthwhile contribution. The translated datasets are also a significant resource for the community. While some existing work has touched on multilingual reasoning and transfer learning, this paper's comprehensive, controlled study and its focus on *long* CoT reasoning distinguishes it.

**Significance:** The findings have practical implications for developing more accessible and equitable LLMs.  The observation that English pivoting is not a universal solution is critical, as it challenges the prevailing assumption that English-centric approaches are sufficient. Highlighting the language-dependency of fine-tuning data requirements is also important for resource-efficient model development. The release of translated datasets fills a gap in the community and should facilitate future work. It points to the nuances and intricacies of applying CoT techniques in multilingual settings, moving beyond a one-size-fits-all mentality.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper systematically investigates various factors affecting multilingual CoT reasoning.
*   **Controlled experiments:** The use of translated datasets and consistent model architectures allows for controlled comparisons.
*   **Practical insights:** The findings have tangible implications for multilingual LLM development.
*   **Dataset release:**  The availability of translated datasets contributes to the community's resources.
*   Addresses a gap in the research: Long CoT reasoning is largely under-explored.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Language Scope:** While the paper includes several languages, the selection isn't fully representative of the world's linguistic diversity.  A more comprehensive linguistic analysis of why certain languages benefit more from specific techniques would have added further depth.
*   **Automatic Translation:** Relying on automatic translation introduces potential noise and biases into the datasets. While Gemini 2.0 Flash is a strong translator, a deeper investigation into translation errors and their impact on model performance would be beneficial. A user study assessing the naturalness of the translations could also increase the validity of the results.
*   **Evaluation Metrics:** Evaluation relies heavily on accuracy. A more nuanced analysis of the types of errors made by the models in different languages could provide further insights. For example, are some models making logical errors or failing to understand the problem?
*   **Model Choice:** While Qwen is a strong series of models, the experiments were only conducted on these architectures.  It is unclear how applicable these findings will be on other model families (e.g., LLaMA).

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a detailed empirical analysis of long CoT reasoning across multiple languages. It challenges assumptions about the universal effectiveness of English pivoting and highlights the importance of language-specific considerations in fine-tuning. The insights regarding data quality/quantity trade-offs and the effects of multilingual pretraining are also valuable. The translated datasets will be a useful resource for future research.

However, the limitations regarding language scope, reliance on automatic translation, evaluation metrics, and a limited selection of model architectures prevents this from being a truly groundbreaking paper. A deeper analysis of the linguistic nuances affecting performance and a more rigorous investigation of potential translation biases would have significantly strengthened the paper.

### Universal and Transferable Adversarial Attack on Large Language Models Using Exponentiated Gradient Descent
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14853v1)
- **Authors**: Sajib Biswas, Mao Nishino, Samuel Jacob Chacko, Xiuwen Liu
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in critical applications, ensuring their robustness and safety alignment remains a major challenge. Despite the overall success of alignment techniques such as reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) on typical prompts, LLMs remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks enabled by crafted adversarial triggers appended to user prompts. Most existing jailbreak methods either rely on inefficient searches over discrete token spaces or direct optimization of continuous embeddings. While continuous embeddings can be given directly to selected open-source models as input, doing so is not feasible for proprietary models. On the other hand, projecting these embeddings back into valid discrete tokens introduces additional complexity and often reduces attack effectiveness. We propose an intrinsic optimization method which directly optimizes relaxed one-hot encodings of the adversarial suffix tokens using exponentiated gradient descent coupled with Bregman projection, ensuring that the optimized one-hot encoding of each token always remains within the probability simplex. We provide theoretical proof of convergence for our proposed method and implement an efficient algorithm that effectively jailbreaks several widely used LLMs. Our method achieves higher success rates and faster convergence compared to three state-of-the-art baselines, evaluated on five open-source LLMs and four adversarial behavior datasets curated for evaluating jailbreak methods. In addition to individual prompt attacks, we also generate universal adversarial suffixes effective across multiple prompts and demonstrate transferability of optimized suffixes to different LLMs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method for generating adversarial attacks (jailbreaks) on Large Language Models (LLMs). The approach uses exponentiated gradient descent (EGD) to optimize relaxed one-hot encodings of the adversarial suffix tokens.  A key aspect is that this optimization intrinsically maintains constraints on the token probabilities, avoiding the need for explicit projection steps required by methods like Projected Gradient Descent (PGD). The authors provide a theoretical convergence proof for their method and demonstrate empirically that it achieves higher success rates and faster convergence compared to state-of-the-art baselines, including GCG, PGD, and SoftPromptThreats, when evaluated on several open-source LLMs and adversarial behavior datasets. Furthermore, the paper explores the generation of universal adversarial suffixes effective across multiple prompts and demonstrating transferability of optimized suffixes to different LLMs, including proprietary models like GPT-3.5.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the use of EGD for optimizing the relaxed one-hot token encodings directly, inherently maintaining the probability simplex constraints. This avoids the projection step commonly found in other methods (like PGD) which simplifies the process and potentially improves efficiency. Compared to token-level discrete search methods (like GCG), it allows simultaneous updates across all token positions, thus speeding up convergence. While EGD itself isn't a new optimization technique, its application in this specific way to LLM jailbreaking, combined with the theoretical convergence guarantee and the empirical results is what gives the paper its primary novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in the LLM field: the vulnerability to jailbreak attacks. By providing a more efficient and effective method for generating adversarial suffixes, it contributes to a better understanding of LLM weaknesses and could inform the development of more robust defense mechanisms. The demonstration of transferability is important since it shows that attacks generated against one model may be effective against others. The ability to produce *universal* adversarial suffixes is also practically important as it suggests that broadly effective attacks may be possible. The work on universal and transferable suffixes is especially impactful as it showcases the broad applicability of the attack and highlights the generalization of vulnerabilities in LLMs. The ability to successfully transfer these attacks to proprietary models like GPT-3.5 further elevates the practical significance of the research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Sound Theoretical Foundation:** The inclusion of a convergence proof adds rigor to the method and provides some guarantees about its behavior.
    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Evaluation:**  The paper presents extensive experiments comparing the proposed method to multiple strong baselines across diverse datasets and models.  This thorough evaluation strengthens the claims made.
    *   **Practical Relevance:**  The demonstration of universal and transferable attacks, and especially the transfer to proprietary models, increases the practical impact.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and the method is clearly explained, facilitating understanding and reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limitations of Convergence Proof:** The convergence proof has specific requirements (differentiable objective with Lipschitz gradient) that might not strictly hold for all LLMs, particularly those with non-smooth activations, as mentioned in the paper itself.
    *   **Reliance on White-box Settings:** The current implementation relies on full access to the model weights, which limits its applicability to black-box scenarios. This is acknowledged by the authors.
    *   **Limited Success on Advanced Models:** The paper mentions limited success in transferring to GPT-4o-mini and Claude. This indicates that newer alignment techniques may be more resistant to this type of attack.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results show better performance than baselines, the improvement, while consistent, isn't always overwhelmingly large. This means it might not be a completely disruptive breakthrough but rather a solid improvement on existing techniques.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in adversarial attacks on LLMs. It could lead to the development of more sophisticated defense mechanisms and inspire further exploration of optimization techniques for generating adversarial examples. The demonstration of transferability and universality will likely motivate further investigation into these aspects.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-supported technique for generating adversarial attacks on LLMs. The theoretical grounding and comprehensive empirical evaluation strengthen its claims. The demonstration of universality and transferability enhances its significance. While there are limitations related to the white-box setting, convergence guarantees for only certain types of models, and transferability to cutting-edge models, the overall contribution is substantial. EGD is not fundamentally new, but its application to LLM attacks and the direct optimization of relaxed one-hot encodings combined with the convergence proof are valuable. It is also important to note the paper acknowledges limitations and sets direction for future research. Therefore, I believe the paper warrants a solid score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Prompting Brain: Neurocognitive Markers of Expertise in Guiding Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14869v1)
- **Authors**: Hend Al-Khalifa, Raneem Almansour, Layan Abdulrahman Alhuasini, Alanood Alsaleh, Mohamad-Hani Temsah, Mohamad-Hani_Temsah, Ashwag Rafea S Alruwaili
- **Abstract**: Prompt engineering has rapidly emerged as a critical skill for effective interaction with large language models (LLMs). However, the cognitive and neural underpinnings of this expertise remain largely unexplored. This paper presents findings from a cross-sectional pilot fMRI study investigating differences in brain functional connectivity and network activity between experts and intermediate prompt engineers. Our results reveal distinct neural signatures associated with higher prompt engineering literacy, including increased functional connectivity in brain regions such as the left middle temporal gyrus and the left frontal pole, as well as altered power-frequency dynamics in key cognitive networks. These findings offer initial insights into the neurobiological basis of prompt engineering proficiency. We discuss the implications of these neurocognitive markers in Natural Language Processing (NLP). Understanding the neural basis of human expertise in interacting with LLMs can inform the design of more intuitive human-AI interfaces, contribute to cognitive models of LLM interaction, and potentially guide the development of AI systems that better align with human cognitive workflows. This interdisciplinary approach aims to bridge the gap between human cognition and machine intelligence, fostering a deeper understanding of how humans learn and adapt to complex AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the neural correlates of expertise in prompt engineering for large language models (LLMs). Using fMRI, the study compares the brain activity and functional connectivity of expert and intermediate prompt engineers during resting-state.  The findings suggest that experts exhibit distinct neural signatures, including increased functional connectivity in language processing and executive function areas (left MTG, left frontal pole), altered power-frequency dynamics in key cognitive networks (Ventral Visual Network, Posterior Default Mode Network, and Left Lateral Parietal Network), and lower fractional amplitude of low-frequency fluctuations (fALFF). The paper discusses the implications of these findings for designing more intuitive human-AI interfaces, developing cognitive models of LLM interaction, and guiding the creation of AI systems that better align with human cognitive processes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper is novel in its approach to linking expertise in prompt engineering – a very recent and rapidly evolving skill – to specific neural correlates. While neuroimaging studies have explored the neural basis of other complex skills like programming, this is, to the authors' knowledge, the first study directly investigating the neural signatures of prompt engineering proficiency. The application of fMRI to understand human-LLM interaction is a significant and timely contribution, given the increasing importance of this field.

**Significance:** The findings, although preliminary, are significant for several reasons:

*   **Human-AI Interaction:** It offers insights into the cognitive processes underlying effective interaction with LLMs, which can inform the design of more intuitive and efficient human-AI interfaces.
*   **Cognitive Modeling:** The identified neural markers can contribute to building more accurate cognitive models of how humans interact with and "program" LLMs.
*   **AI Design:**  Understanding the neural basis of prompt engineering can potentially guide the development of AI systems that are better aligned with human cognitive styles, improving their usability and effectiveness.

**Strengths:**

*   **Timeliness:** Addresses a relevant and important area given the rise of LLMs.
*   **Interdisciplinary Approach:** Integrates neuroscience, cognitive science, and NLP effectively.
*   **Clear Methodology:**  The fMRI methods, though pilot-scale, are well-described.
*   **Actionable Insights:**  The discussion provides concrete suggestions for how the findings can inform NLP and HCI design.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Small Sample Size:**  The N=22 is a major limitation, reducing the statistical power and generalizability of the findings.
*   **Cross-Sectional Design:**  The cross-sectional design cannot establish causality. It's impossible to determine whether the observed neural differences are a cause or consequence of prompt engineering expertise.
*   **PELS Scale Limitations:** The reliance on a custom-developed (though validated) Prompt Engineering Literacy Scale (PELS) with an "exploratory rather than norm-referenced" cutoff score introduces potential subjectivity.
*   **Resting-State Data Only:** Resting-state fMRI provides correlational insights, but task-based fMRI would be needed to directly link neural activity to specific cognitive processes during prompt engineering.
*   **Lack of Control for Confounding Variables:**  While the study controls for neurological or psychiatric disorders, other factors potentially correlated with both expertise and brain activity (e.g., general intelligence, formal education in related fields) could confound the results.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a pioneering effort in an emerging area. The study addresses a pressing need to understand the cognitive and neural basis of human-AI interaction specifically with LLMs. While the small sample size and cross-sectional design seriously limit its conclusions, it offers a first glimpse into a critical area of research. The initial findings of distinct neural signatures for prompt engineering expertise are suggestive, offering a basis for larger, longitudinal studies and for the development of more nuanced cognitive models of human-AI interaction. Therefore, based on the novelty and actionable insights, the paper warrants a score of 6, which is further elevated by the thoroughness of the investigation.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: q-bio.NC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Squeezed Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14871v1)
- **Authors**: Jyotirmai Singh, Samar Khanna, James Burgess
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models typically inject isotropic Gaussian noise, disregarding structure in the data. Motivated by the way quantum squeezed states redistribute uncertainty according to the Heisenberg uncertainty principle, we introduce Squeezed Diffusion Models (SDM), which scale noise anisotropically along the principal component of the training distribution. As squeezing enhances the signal-to-noise ratio in physics, we hypothesize that scaling noise in a data-dependent manner can better assist diffusion models in learning important data features. We study two configurations: (i) a Heisenberg diffusion model that compensates the scaling on the principal axis with inverse scaling on orthogonal directions and (ii) a standard SDM variant that scales only the principal axis. Counterintuitively, on CIFAR-10/100 and CelebA-64, mild antisqueezing - i.e. increasing variance on the principal axis - consistently improves FID by up to 15% and shifts the precision-recall frontier toward higher recall. Our results demonstrate that simple, data-aware noise shaping can deliver robust generative gains without architectural changes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Squeezed Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Squeezed Diffusion Models" (SDMs), a modification to standard diffusion models where the added Gaussian noise is anisotropically scaled along the principal component of the training data.  The motivation comes from quantum squeezing techniques, which redistribute noise to enhance signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) in precision measurements. The authors explore two SDM variants: (1) a "Heisenberg" SDM that maintains constant uncertainty by scaling the principal direction and inversely scaling the orthogonal directions, and (2) a "standard" SDM that scales only the principal direction. Surprisingly, they find that *antisqueezing* (increasing variance) along the principal axis consistently improves FID scores on CIFAR-10/100 and CelebA-64, shifting the precision-recall curve towards higher recall. The paper concludes that data-aware noise shaping, even in this simple form, can improve generative quality without architectural changes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of applying anisotropic noise scaling inspired by quantum squeezing to diffusion models is somewhat novel. Existing work explores data-dependent noise processes, but the specific link to quantum squeezing and the PCA alignment provides a distinct perspective. However, the *implementation* is quite simple. It's essentially a PCA-based scaling of the noise covariance, a technique that is not entirely groundbreaking in itself. Prior work on learned adaptive noise processes in diffusion models weakens the claim of being revolutionary.

*   **Significance:** The finding that *antisqueezing* improves performance is counterintuitive and interesting. This suggests that diffusion models might not always benefit from maximizing SNR during training, and that a carefully chosen noise structure, even if it degrades SNR along some axes, can lead to better generative results. This challenges the "isotropic Gaussian noise is best" assumption that pervades diffusion modeling.
    The improvements in FID are noticeable (up to 15%) and reproducible across datasets. The precision-recall analysis provides some insight into *why* antisqueezing works, linking it to improved recall. However, the improvements are not overwhelmingly large compared to the computational investment in the research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear motivation and connection to quantum squeezing.
    *   Counterintuitive findings (antisqueezing improves performance).
    *   Reproducible improvements across multiple datasets.
    *   Analysis of precision and recall to understand the effect.
    *   Relatively simple modification applicable to existing diffusion models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The implementation is relatively straightforward.
    *   The improvements, while consistent, are not a drastic jump in performance.
    *   The explanation of why antisqueezing works is somewhat speculative, relying on precision and recall arguments without a truly deep theoretical understanding.
    *   The choice of a linear squeezing schedule and PCA in RGB pixel space could be further justified or explored.
    *   The theoretical analysis could benefit from a deeper dive into the effects of the approximation of the posterior as claimed in appendix B.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   The paper can inspire further research into data-aware noise shaping in diffusion models.
    *   The antisqueezing finding might lead to new training strategies that deliberately degrade SNR along certain axes to improve overall generative quality.
    *   The paper demonstrates that insights from other fields (quantum metrology) can be successfully applied to machine learning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a modestly novel idea with a non-trivial experimental investigation showing a counter-intuitive result. The practical improvements are not massive but significant enough to warrant attention. While the novelty of the method is limited, the antisqueezing discovery and its connection to precision-recall offer valuable insights into diffusion model behavior. It has the potential to influence future research directions in noise engineering for generative models.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Quantization Meets dLLMs: A Systematic Study of Post-training Quantization for Diffusion LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14896v1)
- **Authors**: Haokun Lin, Haobo Xu, Yichen Wu, Ziyu Guo, Renrui Zhang, Zhichao Lu, Ying Wei, Qingfu Zhang, Zhenan Sun
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in diffusion large language models (dLLMs) have introduced a promising alternative to autoregressive (AR) LLMs for natural language generation tasks, leveraging full attention and denoising-based decoding strategies. However, the deployment of these models on edge devices remains challenging due to their massive parameter scale and high resource demands. While post-training quantization (PTQ) has emerged as a widely adopted technique for compressing AR LLMs, its applicability to dLLMs remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present the first systematic study on quantizing diffusion-based language models. We begin by identifying the presence of activation outliers, characterized by abnormally large activation values that dominate the dynamic range. These outliers pose a key challenge to low-bit quantization, as they make it difficult to preserve precision for the majority of values. More importantly, we implement state-of-the-art PTQ methods and conduct a comprehensive evaluation across multiple task types and model variants. Our analysis is structured along four key dimensions: bit-width, quantization method, task category, and model type. Through this multi-perspective evaluation, we offer practical insights into the quantization behavior of dLLMs under different configurations. We hope our findings provide a foundation for future research in efficient dLLM deployment. All codes and experimental setups will be released to support the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Quantization Meets dLLMs: A Systematic Study of Post-training Quantization for Diffusion LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper presents the first systematic study of post-training quantization (PTQ) techniques applied to diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs).  It identifies activation outliers in dLLMs as a challenge for quantization, implements state-of-the-art PTQ methods, and comprehensively evaluates them across various tasks, model types (LLaDA and Dream), bit-widths, and quantization methods (GPTQ, AWQ, SmoothQuant, QuaRot, DuQuant). The analysis investigates preferred bit-widths, compares different quantization methods, and explores how task categories and model types influence quantization performance. The authors find that 4-bit quantization is effective for weight-only quantization, while 8-bit is preferable for weight-activation quantization. GPTQ generally outperforms AWQ, while rotation-based methods like DuQuant and QuaRot show advantages over SmoothQuant. Instruction-tuned models are more robust to quantization than base models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in being the *first* dedicated systematic study of post-training quantization specifically tailored for *diffusion-based* large language models.  While quantization itself is not a new technique, its application to the burgeoning field of dLLMs addresses a significant gap in efficient deployment research. Most prior work has focused on autoregressive LLMs, and dLLMs possess distinct characteristics (e.g., full attention, denoising), making direct transfer of existing PTQ methods non-trivial. The identified presence and distribution of activation outliers in dLLMs, different from standard LLMs, further strengthens the novel aspect of the study.

* **Significance:** The paper contributes significantly to the practical deployment of dLLMs, which are currently hindered by high computational costs and memory demands.  The insights provided on suitable bit-widths and optimal quantization methods offer a crucial starting point for researchers and practitioners aiming to compress and accelerate dLLMs.  The identification of task-specific sensitivity to quantization provides valuable guidance for prioritizing tasks during deployment.  By open-sourcing their code and experimental setups, the authors actively foster further research in this area. The comparative analysis of various PTQ methods, highlighting the strengths and weaknesses of each in the context of dLLMs, facilitates informed decision-making for deployment scenarios.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Systematic and Comprehensive:** The paper's main strength is the rigorous, systematic approach to analyzing quantization. The multi-dimensional evaluation (bit-width, method, task, model) provides a holistic view of the problem.
    * **Practical Insights:** The paper offers concrete recommendations (e.g., preferred bit-widths, method comparisons) that can be directly applied.
    * **Well-Defined Research Questions:** The four research questions clearly structure the investigation and contribute to a focused analysis.
    * **Open-Source Contribution:** The authors plan to release their code, which will be invaluable to the community.
    * **Identification of Activation Outliers:** Identifying the presence and analyzing the characteristics of activation outliers within dLLMs is an important contribution, highlighting a key challenge for quantization.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Model Variety:** While LLaDA and Dream are representative dLLMs, expanding the evaluation to include a wider range of architectures would enhance the generalizability of the findings.
    * **Lack of Runtime Performance Analysis:** The paper primarily focuses on accuracy. While mention of acceleration is present, a concrete comparison of the runtime performance of quantized vs. full-precision dLLMs on various hardware would further demonstrate the practical benefits of the techniques.
    * **Limited Exploration of Outlier Mitigation:** While the paper identifies activation outliers, a deeper exploration of strategies to specifically mitigate their impact on quantization performance could add further value. This could include investigation of dynamic quantization, or custom outlier clipping.
    * **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the paper covers a good set of tasks, further extension into generation-specific metrics such as perplexity or BLEU score could further bolster the contribution.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper will likely serve as a foundational reference for future research on efficient dLLM deployment. It will stimulate further investigation into more advanced PTQ techniques, outlier mitigation strategies, and task-specific optimization. It may also inspire hardware vendors to develop optimized kernels for quantized dLLMs.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a significant and novel contribution by systematically studying quantization for dLLMs, a previously unexplored area. The comprehensive evaluation, practical insights, and planned code release position it as a key resource for researchers and practitioners. While there are some limitations (e.g., model variety, runtime analysis), the strengths outweigh these weaknesses. The paper directly addresses a critical challenge in dLLM deployment and lays a solid groundwork for future advancements. While a score of 9 or 10 would require a truly groundbreaking theoretical contribution *and* extensive empirical results, an 8 reflects the strong practical impact and novel application of existing techniques in a previously unstudied field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Improving LLMs for Machine Translation Using Synthetic Preference Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14951v1)
- **Authors**: Dario Vajda, Domen Vreš, Marko Robnik-Šikonja
- **Abstract**: Large language models have emerged as effective machine translation systems. In this paper, we explore how a general instruction-tuned large language model can be improved for machine translation using relatively few easily produced data resources. Using Slovene as a use case, we improve the GaMS-9B-Instruct model using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) training on a programmatically curated and enhanced subset of a public dataset. As DPO requires pairs of quality-ranked instances, we generated its training dataset by translating English Wikipedia articles using two LLMs, GaMS-9B-Instruct and EuroLLM-9B-Instruct. We ranked the resulting translations based on heuristics coupled with automatic evaluation metrics such as COMET. The evaluation shows that our fine-tuned model outperforms both models involved in the dataset generation. In comparison to the baseline models, the fine-tuned model achieved a COMET score gain of around 0.04 and 0.02, respectively, on translating Wikipedia articles. It also more consistently avoids language and formatting errors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of improving machine translation (MT) for less-resourced languages, specifically English to Slovene. It proposes a method that uses Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to fine-tune a large language model (LLM) – GaMS-9B-Instruct. The key innovation lies in generating a synthetic preference dataset by translating English Wikipedia and Common Crawl articles using two different LLMs (GaMS-9B-Instruct and EuroLLM-9B-Instruct) and then ranking these translations based on a combination of heuristics (wrong language, truncation, formatting issues) and automatic evaluation metrics (COMET score). This preference data is then used to train the GaMS-9B-Instruct model using DPO. The authors demonstrate that the fine-tuned model outperforms both base models (GaMS-9B-Instruct and EuroLLM-9B-Instruct) and achieves comparable performance to the much larger GaMS-27B-Instruct model on SloBench, while showing significant improvements in avoiding language errors and truncations on a custom evaluation set.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the automatic generation of a preference dataset for a low-resource language *without* relying on human annotations. While the individual components (using multiple models for translation, heuristics-based filtering, COMET scoring, DPO training) are not entirely new, the *combination* of these techniques for creating a synthetic, yet effective, preference dataset is innovative. It is language agnostic and easily adaptable.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from its practical applicability.  Many low-resource languages lack high-quality parallel corpora or human-annotated preference datasets.  This method offers a cost-effective and scalable approach to improving MT performance in such scenarios. The method directly addresses the problem of creating data to fine-tune LLMs and also deals with the more subtle task of ensuring the translation consistently achieves high performance rather than just "good enough".

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical and Scalable:**  The synthetic data generation pipeline is relatively easy to implement and scale, making it valuable for other low-resource languages. The elimination of human annotation significantly reduces costs.
    *   **Demonstrated Improvement:** The empirical results convincingly show the effectiveness of the approach. The fine-tuned model demonstrably outperforms the base models, especially in terms of avoiding common errors.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper includes evaluations on both a standard benchmark (SloBench) and a custom evaluation set designed to measure performance on longer texts, giving a good overview of the model's strengths and limitations.
    *   **Open Source Contributions:** Sharing the code and model weights facilitates reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Bias:** The method largely relies on Wikipedia and CC-News data, potentially introducing bias towards those specific domains and writing styles. The authors acknowledge this limitation. The composition of generated formatting pair of 20% could be further analyzed and justified.
    *   **Reliance on COMET:**  While COMET is a state-of-the-art metric, it's still an approximation of human judgment. The accuracy of the preference dataset hinges on the reliability of COMET scores, and its bias may influence the fine-tuned model.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** While the paper identifies some specific error types, a more in-depth analysis of the remaining errors would be beneficial.
    *   **SloBench Performance:** While improved, the SloBench performance only matches the larger GaMS-27B-Instruct, indicating room for further improvement. A more thorough evaluation of edge cases and nuanced performance improvement would have been valuable.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   The paper could inspire similar research in other low-resource languages and domains.
    *   The proposed approach could be used to improve the quality of existing open-source MT models.
    *   The publicly available model can serve as a baseline for future research on Slovene MT.
    *   The paper highlights the usefulness of DPO for fine-tuning existing language models, not just training from scratch.

**Overall:** The paper presents a valuable and practical approach to improving machine translation for low-resource languages.  The combination of synthetic data generation with DPO offers a promising alternative to traditional methods.  While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**
The paper demonstrates a practical solution to the challenge of MT in low-resource languages and makes a valuable contribution by offering a clear, easily adoptable methodology. It's a valuable contribution, but has a few limitation concerning limited dataset biases.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Aura-CAPTCHA: A Reinforcement Learning and GAN-Enhanced Multi-Modal CAPTCHA System
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14976v1)
- **Authors**: Joydeep Chandra, Prabal Manhas, Ramanjot Kaur, Rashi Sahay
- **Abstract**: Aura-CAPTCHA was developed as a multi-modal CAPTCHA system to address vulnerabilities in traditional methods that are increasingly bypassed by AI technologies, such as Optical Character Recognition (OCR) and adversarial image processing. The design integrated Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) for generating dynamic image challenges, Reinforcement Learning (RL) for adaptive difficulty tuning, and Large Language Models (LLMs) for creating text and audio prompts. Visual challenges included 3x3 grid selections with at least three correct images, while audio challenges combined randomized numbers and words into a single task. RL adjusted difficulty based on incorrect attempts, response time, and suspicious user behavior. Evaluations on real-world traffic demonstrated a 92% human success rate and a 10% bot bypass rate, significantly outperforming existing CAPTCHA systems. The system provided a robust and scalable approach for securing online applications while remaining accessible to users, addressing gaps highlighted in previous research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Aura-CAPTCHA, a multi-modal CAPTCHA system designed to improve security and accessibility compared to traditional CAPTCHAs. Aura-CAPTCHA leverages Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) to generate dynamic visual and audio challenges, Reinforcement Learning (RL) to adapt the difficulty of the challenges based on user interaction, and Large Language Models (LLMs) for prompt generation. The system analyzes user interactions using a combination of heuristics and machine learning to distinguish between humans and bots. The results of the experiments on real-world traffic suggest that Aura-CAPTCHA has high human success rate and a low bot bypass rate, outperforming existing CAPTCHA systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Multi-modal Approach:**  The use of both visual and audio elements is a good strategy, potentially making the CAPTCHA more resistant to bots designed to solve single-modal challenges.
    * **Adaptive Difficulty:** The integration of Reinforcement Learning to dynamically adjust the difficulty based on user behavior is a significant advantage.  This addresses a common problem with CAPTCHAs – either being too easy for bots or too frustrating for humans. The adaptability feature allows a more balanced and optimized user experience.
    * **GAN-Generated Content:** Using GANs to generate dynamic and unpredictable CAPTCHA content makes it more difficult for bots relying on pre-trained datasets to bypass the system.  This moves away from static or predictable challenges that bots can easily learn.
    * **User Interaction Analysis:**  Employing both heuristics and machine learning to analyze user interaction data (mouse movements, response times, etc.) can improve the accuracy of bot detection, going beyond just correct/incorrect answers.
    * **Evaluation Metrics:** The paper uses relevant metrics (bypass rate, human success rate, response time, false positive rate) to evaluate the performance of Aura-CAPTCHA, allowing for a fair comparison to traditional methods.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty:** The individual components (GANs for content generation, RL for adaptation, and machine learning for user analysis) are not entirely novel. The *combination* of these elements is where the novelty lies, but the paper needs to strongly emphasize this synergy and justify its advantages more clearly.
    * **Implementation details:** While the paper mentions the use of TensorFlow, Scikit-learn, and OpenAI Gym, the precise details of the GAN architecture (e.g., layer structure, training specifics), the RL reward function and state space, and the ML classifier used for user interaction analysis are not described in depth. This makes it difficult to reproduce the results or assess the practical feasibility of the approach.
    * **Accessibility Concerns:** The paper briefly mentions accessibility but does not adequately address potential issues for users with disabilities. Multi-modal systems can be particularly challenging for users with visual or auditory impairments. While it's acknowledged as a future direction, this needs to be more thoroughly considered in the design and evaluation, especially considering that accessibility is a key motivation for CAPTCHAs in the first place.
    * **Comparison to State-of-the-Art:** While the paper compares Aura-CAPTCHA to traditional CAPTCHAs, it would be more compelling to compare against the latest, most sophisticated CAPTCHA systems and bot attack strategies. The field is rapidly evolving, and a comparison to older methods doesn't fully demonstrate the system's robustness. There are numerous research papers addressing bot mitigation, so more appropriate references would add credibility.
    * **Limited Scope of Evaluation:**  The evaluation, while promising, might not be comprehensive enough.  For example, the diversity and adaptability of the GAN-generated content should be thoroughly tested to ensure it doesn't fall into predictable patterns over time. The security of the user analysis part should be deeply evaluated.
    * **The use of LLMs:** While stated in the abstract, the use of LLMs for creating the audio/text prompts is underdeveloped. If not developed and justified, then these prompts would undermine the robustness of the entire CAPTCHA system.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a potentially valuable contribution to CAPTCHA technology by combining GANs, RL, and user interaction analysis. However, the lack of novelty, the need for further implementation details, the underdeveloped evaluation and the limited comparison to state-of-the-art systems lowers its impact. The concerns about accessibility must also be addressed.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Multilingual Datasets for Custom Input Extraction and Explanation Requests Parsing in Conversational XAI Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14982v1)
- **Authors**: Qianli Wang, Tatiana Anikina, Nils Feldhus, Simon Ostermann, Fedor Splitt, Jiaao Li, Yoana Tsoneva, Sebastian Möller, Vera Schmitt
- **Abstract**: Conversational explainable artificial intelligence (ConvXAI) systems based on large language models (LLMs) have garnered considerable attention for their ability to enhance user comprehension through dialogue-based explanations. Current ConvXAI systems often are based on intent recognition to accurately identify the user's desired intention and map it to an explainability method. While such methods offer great precision and reliability in discerning users' underlying intentions for English, a significant challenge in the scarcity of training data persists, which impedes multilingual generalization. Besides, the support for free-form custom inputs, which are user-defined data distinct from pre-configured dataset instances, remains largely limited. To bridge these gaps, we first introduce MultiCoXQL, a multilingual extension of the CoXQL dataset spanning five typologically diverse languages, including one low-resource language. Subsequently, we propose a new parsing approach aimed at enhancing multilingual parsing performance, and evaluate three LLMs on MultiCoXQL using various parsing strategies. Furthermore, we present Compass, a new multilingual dataset designed for custom input extraction in ConvXAI systems, encompassing 11 intents across the same five languages as MultiCoXQL. We conduct monolingual, cross-lingual, and multilingual evaluations on Compass, employing three LLMs of varying sizes alongside BERT-type models.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**
The paper addresses the limitations of current Conversational Explainable AI (ConvXAI) systems, specifically the lack of multilingual support and the inability to handle custom user inputs. To overcome these challenges, the authors introduce two novel datasets: MultiCoXQL, a multilingual extension of the CoXQL dataset for intent recognition, and Compass, a new multilingual dataset designed for custom input extraction in ConvXAI systems. The paper evaluates several existing parsing strategies and proposes a novel Guided Multi-prompt Parsing (GMP) approach to enhance multilingual parsing performance. The performance of various LLMs and BERT-type models is evaluated on both datasets under monolingual, cross-lingual, and multilingual settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**
*   **Novelty:** The introduction of MultiCoXQL and Compass is a significant contribution to the field. The expansion of CoXQL to multiple languages addresses a crucial gap in multilingual XAI. The Compass dataset is particularly novel as it enables ConvXAI systems to handle custom user inputs, moving beyond pre-configured dataset instances. The proposed Guided Multi-prompt Parsing (GMP) approach also demonstrates novelty by combining the strengths of existing techniques to improve multilingual parsing accuracy.
*   **Significance:** The datasets and methods presented in the paper have the potential to significantly advance the development and application of ConvXAI systems. Multilingual support is essential for making XAI accessible to a broader audience. The ability to handle custom inputs enhances the flexibility and usability of ConvXAI systems, enabling more personalized and engaging user experiences. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approaches in multilingual settings.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-structured and provides a clear explanation of the problem, the proposed solutions, and the experimental setup.
    *   The creation of MultiCoXQL and Compass datasets addresses a critical gap in the availability of multilingual resources for ConvXAI.
    *   The proposed Guided Multi-prompt Parsing (GMP) approach shows promising results in enhancing multilingual parsing accuracy.
    *   The extensive experimental evaluation provides valuable insights into the performance of different models and approaches under various settings.
    *   The error analysis provides further understanding and insight to guide future research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The quality of the machine translation used to create MultiCoXQL and Compass datasets relies on the performance of the machine translation model, Gemini-1.5-pro, which may introduce potential inaccuracies.
    *   The annotation and evaluation of the datasets could be further strengthened by including inter-annotator agreement for all languages.
    *   The paper does not implement all the proposed operations, and their integration into ConvXAI systems is left for future work.
    *   The evaluation is limited to a specific set of models and approaches, and further research is needed to explore the performance of other techniques.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The MultiCoXQL and Compass datasets are likely to become valuable resources for the ConvXAI research community, fostering the development of more effective and accessible systems.
    *   The Guided Multi-prompt Parsing (GMP) approach could serve as a baseline for future research on multilingual parsing in ConvXAI.
    *   The findings from the experimental evaluation could guide the selection of appropriate models and approaches for specific tasks and languages.

Overall, this is a valuable and well-executed study that addresses significant limitations in current ConvXAI systems.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents valuable contributions through the introduction of two novel datasets and a promising parsing approach. While the machine translation quality is a potential concern, the human evaluation and correction mitigate this issue. The paper also provides a thorough empirical analysis of various models, which is beneficial for future research. The limitations mentioned, such as the lack of implementation of all proposed operations and complete inter-annotator agreement metrics, prevent it from receiving a higher score. Nevertheless, the paper’s contribution to the field is undeniable, and it will likely stimulate further research in multilingual ConvXAI.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### TAIGen: Training-Free Adversarial Image Generation via Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15020v1)
- **Authors**: Susim Roy, Anubhooti Jain, Mayank Vatsa, Richa Singh
- **Abstract**: Adversarial attacks from generative models often produce low-quality images and require substantial computational resources. Diffusion models, though capable of high-quality generation, typically need hundreds of sampling steps for adversarial generation. This paper introduces TAIGen, a training-free black-box method for efficient adversarial image generation. TAIGen produces adversarial examples using only 3-20 sampling steps from unconditional diffusion models. Our key finding is that perturbations injected during the mixing step interval achieve comparable attack effectiveness without processing all timesteps. We develop a selective RGB channel strategy that applies attention maps to the red channel while using GradCAM-guided perturbations on green and blue channels. This design preserves image structure while maximizing misclassification in target models. TAIGen maintains visual quality with PSNR above 30 dB across all tested datasets. On ImageNet with VGGNet as source, TAIGen achieves 70.6% success against ResNet, 80.8% against MNASNet, and 97.8% against ShuffleNet. The method generates adversarial examples 10x faster than existing diffusion-based attacks. Our method achieves the lowest robust accuracy, indicating it is the most impactful attack as the defense mechanism is least successful in purifying the images generated by TAIGen.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TAIGen: Training-Free Adversarial Image Generation via Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TAIGen, a novel training-free black-box adversarial attack method leveraging diffusion models.  TAIGen aims to efficiently generate adversarial examples by only requiring a few sampling steps (3-20) from an unconditional diffusion model. The core idea is to inject perturbations strategically within a specific interval of the diffusion process, identified as a "mixing step" where the latent Gaussian distribution converges towards the data distribution. The method further employs a selective RGB channel strategy, using attention maps for the red channel and GradCAM-guided perturbations for the green and blue channels to both preserve image structure and maximize misclassification.  The authors demonstrate TAIGen's effectiveness across several datasets (CIFAR-10, CelebA-HQ, ImageNet) and target models, showing high attack success rates, visual quality (PSNR), and faster generation times compared to existing diffusion-based attacks. They also demonstrate a lower robust accuracy, suggesting a potent attack against defense mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:

    *   **Few-step adversarial generation:** The idea of targeting a specific "mixing step" interval instead of using all timesteps significantly improves efficiency and is a key contribution. Prior diffusion-based attack papers used much more sampling.
    *   **Selective RGB channel perturbation:** The combination of attention maps and GradCAM guidance across different RGB channels is an interesting strategy to balance perturbation effectiveness and image quality.  It shows a thoughtful understanding of how the model interprets the data and where perturbations have the largest effect.
    *   **Training-free approach:** TAIGen is appealing as it doesn't require training a separate model or fine-tuning the diffusion model for adversarial generation.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Efficiency:** The efficiency of TAIGen is a major advantage. Reducing the number of sampling steps makes adversarial generation more practical, particularly in resource-constrained scenarios.
    *   **Black-box applicability:**  The black-box nature broadens the applicability of the attack, as it doesn't require detailed knowledge of the target model's architecture or gradients.
    *   **Robustness against defenses:**  The claim of achieving the lowest robust accuracy suggests that TAIGen poses a strong challenge to existing defense mechanisms.  This is crucial for highlighting vulnerabilities and encouraging the development of more effective defenses.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined methodology with clear justifications for each component.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across diverse datasets and models.
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating the effectiveness of TAIGen.
    *   Addresses a significant problem: the computational cost of diffusion-based adversarial attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The choice of mixing timesteps could be more rigorously justified. Although they compute the radius shift, a more detailed explanation of *why* those specific steps are most effective would strengthen the argument.
    *   The explanation of why the attack performs weaker in a white-box setting is speculative and could benefit from deeper analysis.
    *   While the method claims to be "training-free," it relies on pre-trained diffusion models and target classifiers. It could be clarified whether transferability between diffusion models is feasible.
    *  The reliance on linear interpolation for downscaling the attention maps could introduce artifacts or limit performance. Investigating more sophisticated resizing techniques would enhance the approach.
    *   The paper is missing an in-depth discussion of the limitations of TAIGen. For instance, what kinds of models or datasets are especially resistant to it? What image characteristics make it more or less effective?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field by enabling more efficient and practical adversarial attacks. It can drive research towards more robust defense mechanisms and a better understanding of the vulnerabilities of deep learning models. The few-step approach could also inspire new adversarial training techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

TAIGen presents a notable advance in diffusion-based adversarial attacks due to its efficiency and training-free nature. The strategy of targeting the mixing step and selectively perturbing RGB channels is innovative and well-supported by empirical results. However, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of its limitations, more rigorous justification of the specific timesteps chosen, and more clarity regarding the choice of auxiliary components and their influence on the method.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### In-Context Iterative Policy Improvement for Dynamic Manipulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15021v1)
- **Authors**: Mark Van der Merwe, Devesh Jha
- **Abstract**: Attention-based architectures trained on internet-scale language data have demonstrated state of the art reasoning ability for various language-based tasks, such as logic problems and textual reasoning. Additionally, these Large Language Models (LLMs) have exhibited the ability to perform few-shot prediction via in-context learning, in which input-output examples provided in the prompt are generalized to new inputs.
