### SparK: Query-Aware Unstructured Sparsity with Recoverable KV Cache Channel Pruning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15212v1)
- **Authors**: Huanxuan Liao, Yixing Xu, Shizhu He, Guanchen Li, Xuanwu Yin, Dong Li, Emad Barsoum, Jun Zhao, Kang Liu
- **Abstract**: Long-context inference in large language models (LLMs) is increasingly constrained by the KV cache bottleneck: memory usage grows linearly with sequence length, while attention computation scales quadratically. Existing approaches address this issue by compressing the KV cache along the temporal axis through strategies such as token eviction or merging to reduce memory and computational overhead. However, these methods often neglect fine-grained importance variations across feature dimensions (i.e., the channel axis), thereby limiting their ability to effectively balance efficiency and model accuracy. In reality, we observe that channel saliency varies dramatically across both queries and positions: certain feature channels carry near-zero information for a given query, while others spike in relevance. To address this oversight, we propose SPARK, a training-free plug-and-play method that applies unstructured sparsity by pruning KV at the channel level, while dynamically restoring the pruned entries during attention score computation. Notably, our approach is orthogonal to existing KV compression and quantization techniques, making it compatible for integration with them to achieve further acceleration. By reducing channel-level redundancy, SPARK enables processing of longer sequences within the same memory budget. For sequences of equal length, SPARK not only preserves or improves model accuracy but also reduces KV cache storage by over 30% compared to eviction-based methods. Furthermore, even with an aggressive pruning ratio of 80%, SPARK maintains performance with less degradation than 5% compared to the baseline eviction method, demonstrating its robustness and effectiveness. Our code will be available at https://github.com/Xnhyacinth/SparK.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper "SPARK: Query-Aware Unstructured Sparsity with Recoverable KV Cache Channel Pruning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPARK, a training-free method for compressing the Key-Value (KV) cache in Large Language Models (LLMs) during inference. SPARK leverages unstructured sparsity by pruning channels at the channel level in the KV cache, based on a query-aware saliency metric.  Critically, SPARK includes a mechanism to recover the contributions of pruned channels during attention score computation by approximating the missing information using cached distributional statistics. The method aims to reduce memory usage and improve inference speed, claiming compatibility with existing KV compression techniques. Experiments on various benchmarks and LLMs demonstrate that SPARK reduces KV cache storage and maintains, or even improves, model accuracy compared to eviction-based methods, especially under aggressive pruning ratios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The approach has some novelty. The idea of channel-wise pruning of KV cache is not brand new (THINK explores structured pruning), but SPARK introduces unstructured pruning with an explicit recovery mechanism. The query-aware saliency metric, while not deeply sophisticated, allows for dynamic adaptation of sparsity patterns which distinguishes it.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very important problem in LLM deployment: the KV cache bottleneck. Making long-context LLMs more efficient is crucial for wider adoption. However, its significance is somewhat mitigated by its limited innovation compared to the existing methods.  The experimental results suggest that it is better than some existing methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Training-free & Plug-and-play: SPARK is attractive as it can be used with existing pre-trained models without fine-tuning or retraining, and can be potentially combined with different approaches that optimize the temporal and spatial axis. This is a major advantage for real-world application.
    *   Recovery Mechanism: The recovery mechanism using distributional statistics is a key differentiator, preserving performance under high pruning ratios compared to methods that simply discard channels. This is supported by experimental results.
    *   Experimental Validation: The paper presents extensive experimental results across multiple benchmarks and LLMs. It compares SPARK with a variety of baselines and examines its performance under different configurations, such as different pruning ratios, and cache sizes.
    *   Strong Empirical Improvement: The experiments show that SPARK can reduce KV cache size by over 30% compared to eviction-based methods while preserving or improving accuracy. Maintaining performance with an 80% pruning ratio is impressive.
    *   Compatible with Prior Optimization Directions: One of the stronger points of the method is that it's orthogonal to and compatible with optimization methods along other axis (such as token eviction/merging, or spatial axis optimization).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited Innovation: While the combination of unstructured sparsity and recovery is beneficial, the individual components are relatively simple and build on existing research.
    *   Saliency Metric Simplification: The proxy saliency score (product of norms) is a simple approximation of the true importance of a channel. Further refinements to the metric, to more closely reflect attention weights could improve performance.
    *   Recovery Mechanism Limitations: The recovery mechanism relies on approximating the contributions of pruned channels using distributional statistics. There could be cases where this approximation performs poorly. A better approach to prune the key and value cache at the same token, but with different attention, seems ideal but may be difficult to implement
    *   Runtime Overhead Concerns: Although claimed to be negligible runtime overhead, the computations required for saliency estimation and pruned channel entry reconstruction will definitely incur overhead in practical scenarios. There might be concerns for very low latency applications, and this overhead could have been thoroughly described.
    *   Evaluation of "On-the-fly" Recovery: The paper doesn't focus on the runtime overhead brought by on-the-fly recovery during decoding. And the result of throughput is not very convincible.

*   **Potential Influence:**  SPARK has the potential to influence future research on KV cache compression by demonstrating the effectiveness of unstructured sparsity and recovery mechanisms. Its training-free nature makes it readily adaptable to existing LLMs. But due to the simplified saliency, the impact is limited.
**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a practically valuable method for compressing the KV cache without requiring retraining, that combines a few existing idea. SPARK offers a good performance improvement. The significance is tied to its ability to maintain performance while greatly reducing memory footprint in practical LLM deployment. Although with the simplistic saliency and limited runtime overhead analysis, making its actual impact is limited.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Select to Know: An Internal-External Knowledge Self-Selection Framework for Domain-Specific Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15213v1)
- **Authors**: Bolei He, Xinran He, Run Shao, Shanfu Shu, Xianwei Xue, Mingquan Cheng, Haifeng Li, Zhenhua Ling
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) perform well in general QA but often struggle in domain-specific scenarios. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) introduces external knowledge but suffers from hallucinations and latency due to noisy retrievals. Continued pretraining internalizes domain knowledge but is costly and lacks cross-domain flexibility. We attribute this challenge to the long-tail distribution of domain knowledge, which leaves partial yet useful internal knowledge underutilized. We further argue that knowledge acquisition should be progressive, mirroring human learning: first understanding concepts, then applying them to complex reasoning. To address this, we propose Selct2Know (S2K), a cost-effective framework that internalizes domain knowledge through an internal-external knowledge self-selection strategy and selective supervised fine-tuning. We also introduce a structured reasoning data generation pipeline and integrate GRPO to enhance reasoning ability. Experiments on medical, legal, and financial QA benchmarks show that S2K consistently outperforms existing methods and matches domain-pretrained LLMs with significantly lower cost.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Select to Know: An Internal-External Knowledge Self-Selection Framework for Domain-Specific Question Answering":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of domain-specific question answering (DSQA) in large language models (LLMs). It argues that LLMs struggle in this area due to the long-tail distribution of domain knowledge, leading to underutilization of partially internalized knowledge. The paper proposes a framework called Select2Know (S2K), which combines internal parametric knowledge with external domain knowledge through a token-level self-selection strategy. S2K also uses selective supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to focus the model on unfamiliar domain knowledge and incorporates Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to enhance reasoning abilities. Experiments across medical, law, and finance domains demonstrate that S2K outperforms existing methods and achieves performance comparable to domain-pretrained LLMs, but at a significantly lower training cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper has some novel aspects, particularly in its specific combination of existing techniques and its focus on self-selection. The token-level internal-external knowledge fusion is a decent idea.

*   **Token-Level Knowledge Fusion:** The token-level fusion and selective SFT are good. This granular approach allows the model to capitalize on its existing, perhaps imperfect, domain understanding rather than blindly relying on retrieved knowledge.

**Significance and Impact:**

*   **Cost-Effectiveness:**  The claim of achieving comparable performance to domain-pretrained models with significantly lower training costs is potentially a high-impact contribution. This could make domain adaptation more accessible.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** All the components (self-selection, selective fine-tuning, GRPO, RAG) are existing, so is the implementation. The data generation is also standard.
*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The experiments focus on only three domains.
*   **Overclaiming:** Stating in the paper that S2K matches domain-pretrained LLMs with significantly lower cost, and in figure 3, you can see that domain-pretrained LLMs are better and have more resources.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a good combination of techniques for domain adaptation of LLMs. The cost-effectiveness is a strong argument in its favor. However, the individual components are based on existing work, and the overall novelty is incremental. The claim in Figure 3 and the paper are inconsistent.

### Self-Guided Function Calling in Large Language Models via Stepwise Experience Recall
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15214v1)
- **Authors**: Sijia Cui, Aiyao He, Shuai Xu, Hongming Zhang, Yanna Wang, Qingyang Zhang, Yajing Wang, Bo Xu
- **Abstract**: Function calling enables large language models (LLMs) to interact with external systems by leveraging tools and APIs. When faced with multi-step tool usage, LLMs still struggle with tool selection, parameter generation, and tool-chain planning. Existing methods typically rely on manually designing task-specific demonstrations, or retrieving from a curated library. These approaches demand substantial expert effort and prompt engineering becomes increasingly complex and inefficient as tool diversity and task difficulty scale. To address these challenges, we propose a self-guided method, Stepwise Experience Recall (SEER), which performs fine-grained, stepwise retrieval from a continually updated experience pool. Instead of relying on static or manually curated library, SEER incrementally augments the experience pool with past successful trajectories, enabling continuous expansion of the pool and improved model performance over time. Evaluated on the ToolQA benchmark, SEER achieves an average improvement of 6.1\% on easy and 4.7\% on hard questions. We further test SEER on $\tau$-bench, which includes two real-world domains. Powered by Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen2.5-72B models, SEER demonstrates substantial accuracy gains of 7.44\% and 23.38\%, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Self-Guided Function Calling in Large Language Models via Stepwise Experience Recall":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Stepwise Experience Recall (SEER), a novel method that enhances the multi-step tool-use capabilities of LLMs. SEER dynamically selects relevant examples from a continually updated experience pool by jointly considering task similarity, toolchain coverage, and intent alignment.  Instead of relying on static or manually curated libraries, SEER incrementally augments the experience pool with past successful trajectories, enabling continuous expansion of the pool and improved model performance over time. The method is evaluated on ToolQA and T-bench benchmarks, showing substantial accuracy gains compared to existing methods.  A key aspect of SEER is its self-guided learning mechanism where successful trajectories are added to the experience pool, reducing reliance on manual annotation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the dynamic and fine-grained retrieval mechanism for in-context learning.  While in-context learning and function calling are well-established, SEER distinguishes itself by focusing on multi-step scenarios and adaptively building an experience pool based on successful interactions. The three-pronged scoring approach combining task similarity, toolchain alignment, and intent recognition is a good way to select relevant examples from a growing trajectory pool. Also, the addition of self-guided experience accumulation adds a new dimension. However, the individual components (trajectory embeddings, intent classification) are not groundbreaking individually.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation of LLMs in real-world applications, where multi-step tool usage is often necessary. By improving LLMs' ability to plan tool chains and execute them effectively, SEER contributes to making these models more practical and useful.  The self-improvement aspect also has the potential to reduce the need for extensive manual data curation and annotation. The substantial improvements on benchmarks like ToolQA and T-bench demonstrate the effectiveness of SEER.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The method is well-motivated, addressing a clear challenge in tool-augmented LLMs.
    *   The stepwise experience recall mechanism is a practical and effective approach for multi-step tool usage.
    *   The self-guided learning loop allows the model to adapt to new tasks and evolving user demands without requiring constant manual updates.
    *   Comprehensive experimental results on relevant benchmarks (ToolQA, T-bench) demonstrate the effectiveness of SEER.
    *   Ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of SEER.
    *   The approach does not require additional training.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation relies heavily on existing benchmarks, which may not fully capture the complexity of real-world tool-use scenarios. It's unclear how well SEER would generalize to entirely new tasks with novel tools.
    *   The reliance on LLM's self-assessment might introduce biases or inaccuracies into the experience pool.  This evaluation process could lead to reinforcing suboptimal behaviors.
    *   While self-improvement is demonstrated, long-term stability and convergence are not thoroughly analyzed. There is a potential risk of the model getting trapped in local optima or experiencing catastrophic forgetting.
    *   The paper assumes that a set of possible intents are known a priori. This might not always be true in an open world.
    * The paper needs further detail in its description of both intent inference and the LLM evaluator.

*   **Potential Influence:**  SEER provides a promising direction for improving the practical utility of LLMs.  It could influence future research on in-context learning, tool learning, and self-improvement in LLMs. It might also inspire the development of more robust and adaptive AI agents for real-world applications. The simple framework lends itself well to further developments.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated approach to a significant problem in tool-augmented LLMs. The use of a dynamically updated experience pool and the multi-dimensional scoring strategy offer a practical way to improve multi-step tool usage. The self-improvement aspect adds considerable value. However, the limitations in evaluation, the reliance on potentially biased self-assessment, and the lack of analysis on long-term behavior slightly temper the overall impact.

### Are Checklists Really Useful for Automatic Evaluation of Generative Tasks?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15218v1)
- **Authors**: Momoka Furuhashi, Kouta Nakayama, Takashi Kodama, Saku Sugawara
- **Abstract**: Automatic evaluation of generative tasks using large language models faces challenges due to ambiguous criteria. Although automatic checklist generation is a potentially promising approach, its usefulness remains underexplored. We investigate whether checklists should be used for all questions or selectively, generate them using six methods, evaluate their effectiveness across eight model sizes, and identify checklist items that correlate with human evaluations. Through experiments on pairwise comparison and direct scoring tasks, we find that selective checklist use tends to improve evaluation performance in pairwise settings, while its benefits are less consistent in direct scoring. Our analysis also shows that even checklist items with low correlation to human scores often reflect human-written criteria, indicating potential inconsistencies in human evaluation. These findings highlight the need to more clearly define objective evaluation criteria to guide both human and automatic evaluations. \footnote{Our code is available at~https://github.com/momo0817/checklist-effectiveness-study
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Are Checklists Really Useful for Automatic Evaluation of Generative Tasks?":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of checklist-based automatic evaluation for generative tasks using Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the challenges of ambiguous evaluation criteria and unstable correlation between LLM evaluation and human judgement. The authors explore *when* checklists are necessary, *how* to create them, and *which* checklist items contribute to alignment with human evaluation. Through controlled experiments on pairwise comparison and direct scoring tasks, they find that selective checklist use can improve performance in pairwise settings, but benefits are less consistent in direct scoring. The analysis also reveals that even checklist items with low correlation to human scores can reflect human-written criteria, suggesting inconsistencies in human evaluations and highlighting the need for clearer, more objective evaluation standards.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper addresses a very relevant and timely issue in the field of LLM evaluation. While checklist-based evaluation isn't entirely new, the work makes a significant contribution by rigorously examining *when* and *how* checklists should be used, rather than simply assuming their universal benefit. The investigation into the types of checklist items that correlate with human evaluations is valuable for understanding the nuances of LLM-based assessment.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The study employs a well-designed set of experiments, using a variety of models, datasets, and checklist generation methods. This increases the robustness and generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The authors provide a detailed quantitative and qualitative analysis of their results. They go beyond simple performance metrics and delve into the specifics of checklist item effectiveness and alignment with human judgments.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers practical guidance for researchers and practitioners involved in LLM evaluation. The findings suggest that a one-size-fits-all approach to checklist use isn't optimal and that careful consideration should be given to the task, model, and checklist generation method.
    *   **Highlights potential problems with Human evaluations.** The study suggest that inconsistencies can stem from the subjective nature of human evaluations
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Language Scope:** The experiments are limited to English datasets, which raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to other languages.
    *   **Specific LLM Focus:** While the models are diverse, they are still a limited representation of the ever-evolving LLM landscape. The results might need to be re-evaluated as newer models emerge.
    *   **Subjective Human Evaluation Still A Factor:** Human evaluation inconsistencies are mentioned, but the paper's scope limits exploration of how to correct human evaluators, instead of just the automatic methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by encouraging a more critical and nuanced approach to automatic LLM evaluation. The recommendations for selective checklist use and the analysis of checklist item characteristics can inform the development of more effective and reliable evaluation methods. The findings also suggest a need for further research into the subjective nature of human evaluations, as well as creating more clear evaluation standards.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper provides a solid contribution to the field of LLM evaluation by challenging the assumed benefits of checklist-based methods and offering practical guidance for their use. The rigorous experimental design and detailed analysis are strengths. However, the limited language scope and potential for human bias are weaknesses that limit the overall impact of the work. Further research to address these limitations would significantly enhance the paper's significance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### See it. Say it. Sorted: Agentic System for Compositional Diagram Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15222v1)
- **Authors**: Hantao Zhang, Jingyang Liu, Ed Li
- **Abstract**: We study sketch-to-diagram generation: converting rough hand sketches into precise, compositional diagrams. Diffusion models excel at photorealism but struggle with the spatial precision, alignment, and symbolic structure required for flowcharts. We introduce See it. Say it. Sorted., a training-free agentic system that couples a Vision-Language Model (VLM) with Large Language Models (LLMs) to produce editable Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) programs. The system runs an iterative loop in which a Critic VLM proposes a small set of qualitative, relational edits; multiple candidate LLMs synthesize SVG updates with diverse strategies (conservative->aggressive, alternative, focused); and a Judge VLM selects the best candidate, ensuring stable improvement. This design prioritizes qualitative reasoning over brittle numerical estimates, preserves global constraints (e.g., alignment, connectivity), and naturally supports human-in-the-loop corrections. On 10 sketches derived from flowcharts in published papers, our method more faithfully reconstructs layout and structure than two frontier closed-source image generation LLMs (GPT-5 and Gemini-2.5-Pro), accurately composing primitives (e.g., multi-headed arrows) without inserting unwanted text. Because outputs are programmatic SVGs, the approach is readily extensible to presentation tools (e.g., PowerPoint) via APIs and can be specialized with improved prompts and task-specific tools. The codebase is open-sourced at https://github.com/hantaoZhangrichard/see_it_say_it_sorted.git.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "See it. Say it. Sorted," a novel, training-free agentic system for compositional diagram generation. The system converts hand-drawn sketches into precise, editable SVG diagrams. It uses a Vision-Language Model (VLM) as a critic and Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate candidate SVG modifications.  The key is an iterative Critic-Candidates-Judge loop: the VLM critiques discrepancies between the sketch and the current diagram, multiple LLMs propose diverse SVG modifications based on that critique, and then the VLM judges and selects the best candidate. This approach prioritizes qualitative reasoning and global constraints over precise numerical estimations.  The authors demonstrate that their system outperforms frontier image generation LLMs (GPT-5 and Gemini-2.5-Pro) in reconstructing the structure and characteristics of flowcharts from sketches. The output is in SVG format, enabling easy editing and integration into presentation tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in the combination of a VLM critic, LLM candidate generators, and a VLM judge within an iterative loop *without requiring training*. Existing work either trains models directly on vector graphics or relies on VLMs for individual tasks.  The architecture itself is somewhat novel but draws on existing agentic system design principles.  The key advantage is the "training-free" aspect, making it readily adaptable.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of diffusion-based generative models for diagram creation – their difficulty in handling spatial precision, alignment, and symbolic structure. By outputting editable SVG code, the system offers significant advantages in terms of adaptability, interpretability, and integration with existing graphics design environments.  This moves beyond simple pixel-level synthesis. The results demonstrate a substantial improvement over existing image generation models in the specific task of diagram creation, suggesting a potentially significant impact. However, the limited evaluation to only 10 flowchart examples, while convincing, limits the generalizability of the findings to broader diagram types.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Training-free approach:**  Avoids the costly and data-intensive process of training a specialized model.
    * **Emphasis on qualitative reasoning:**  Leverages VLMs' strengths in understanding spatial relationships.
    * **Iterative refinement:** The Critic-Candidates-Judge loop allows for stable convergence towards the target diagram.
    * **SVG output:** Enables easy editing, integration, and scalability.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited evaluation:** The evaluation relies on a small set of sketches derived from flowcharts, which may not be representative of all types of diagrams.
    * **Reliance on VLMs and LLMs:** The performance of the system is heavily dependent on the underlying VLMs and LLMs. Future improvements in these models will directly benefit the system.
    * **Limited complexity:** The set of primitive shapes is minimal, potentially limiting the complexity of generated diagrams.
    * **Lack of error analysis:** The paper lacks detailed error analysis regarding the types of errors the system is prone to, which could help in improving the system.
* **Potential Impact:** The work has the potential to influence the field of generative graphics by providing a practical and adaptable solution for diagram creation. The focus on qualitative reasoning and global constraints could inspire new approaches to structured image synthesis. The extensibility of the system through APIs and task-specific tools makes it promising for real-world applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel system that addresses a real-world problem with a practical and adaptable solution. The training-free approach and the focus on qualitative reasoning are significant strengths. The experimental results, although limited, provide convincing evidence of the system's effectiveness. However, the limited evaluation and the reliance on underlying VLMs and LLMs prevent the paper from receiving a higher score. Overall, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field of generative graphics and has the potential to influence future research in this area.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### GenTune: Toward Traceable Prompts to Improve Controllability of Image Refinement in Environment Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15227v1)
- **Authors**: Wen-Fan Wang, Ting-Ying Lee, Chien-Ting Lu, Che-Wei Hsu, Nil Ponsa Campany, Yu Chen, Mike Y. Chen, Bing-Yu Chen
- **Abstract**: Environment designers in the entertainment industry create imaginative 2D and 3D scenes for games, films, and television, requiring both fine-grained control of specific details and consistent global coherence. Designers have increasingly integrated generative AI into their workflows, often relying on large language models (LLMs) to expand user prompts for text-to-image generation, then iteratively refining those prompts and applying inpainting. However, our formative study with 10 designers surfaced two key challenges: (1) the lengthy LLM-generated prompts make it difficult to understand and isolate the keywords that must be revised for specific visual elements; and (2) while inpainting supports localized edits, it can struggle with global consistency and correctness. Based on these insights, we present GenTune, an approach that enhances human--AI collaboration by clarifying how AI-generated prompts map to image content. Our GenTune system lets designers select any element in a generated image, trace it back to the corresponding prompt labels, and revise those labels to guide precise yet globally consistent image refinement. In a summative study with 20 designers, GenTune significantly improved prompt--image comprehension, refinement quality, and efficiency, and overall satisfaction (all $p < .01$) compared to current practice. A follow-up field study with two studios further demonstrated its effectiveness in real-world settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "GenTune: Toward Traceable Prompts to Improve Controllability of Image Refinement in Environment Design" addresses the challenge of refining AI-generated images for environment design, specifically focusing on improving controllability and understanding of how prompts relate to visual elements.  It introduces GenTune, a system featuring two key modules: (1) Traceable Prompts, allowing designers to select an area of interest in the image and reveal the corresponding prompt label, and (2) Semantic-Guided Refinement, which supports targeted edits via natural language or reference images, with options for global refinement, semantic-guided prompt refinement with controlled seed, and semantic-guided inpainting.  The authors present a formative study, a summative study with 20 participants, and a field study with two studios, demonstrating that GenTune significantly improves prompt-image comprehension, refinement quality and efficiency, and overall satisfaction compared to existing practices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper introduces a novel and practical approach to refining AI-generated images by providing a human-centered system focusing on prompt interpretability and controllability in a specific domain: environment design. The traceable prompt is a well-designed feature enhancing understanding between the prompt and the generated image, along with the semantic guided refinement options that helps designers with visual elements with precise refinements, reducing the need for trial and error. While some existing systems address prompt editing or image refinement, the combination of traceable prompts, semantic-guided refinement with different refinement modes, and the targeted application to environment design distinguishes GenTune.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and growing challenge as generative AI is increasingly integrated into creative workflows. Environment design, a complex task involving both global coherence and fine-grained detail, benefits significantly from enhanced controllability. The formative study clearly identifies the needs of environment designers, and the summative and field studies offer strong evidence that GenTune effectively addresses these needs, improving efficiency and satisfaction. The paper's contribution is significant because it moves beyond simply generating images to facilitating a more controlled and understandable refinement process for creative professionals.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly articulates the problem, the challenges faced by environment designers using GenAI, and the specific needs that GenTune addresses.
    *   **Human-Centered Design:** The design of GenTune is informed by a thorough formative study, ensuring that the system is tailored to the workflows and requirements of its target users.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper includes a comprehensive evaluation with both within-subject experiment, an open-ended task, and field studies, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of GenTune.
    *   **Practicality:** The system's design and the positive results from the field study suggest that GenTune can be readily integrated into real-world production pipelines.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, clearly explains the system's design and functionality, and provides sufficient details about the evaluation methodology.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on LLMs and Image Models:** Like all systems that leverage generative AI, GenTune's performance is dependent on the underlying LLMs and image models. Limitations in these models, such as structural inconsistencies or a lack of fine-grained control, can impact the overall effectiveness of GenTune.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** While the approach is promising, the evaluation is focused on environment design. While the concept of traceability is useful, the specific labels used might not apply to other generation domains. The authors could consider a broader assessment or a discussion of how the approach could be adapted for other creative tasks.
    *   **Ethical considerations:** The paper discusses the impact of GenAI on artist workforce but lacks in discussion how traceability enhances the responsibility of AI work, which could be helpful to reduce bias.

*   **Potential Influence:** GenTune's approach of traceable prompts and semantic-guided refinement has the potential to influence the design of future human-AI collaboration tools for creative tasks. The paper's findings can inform the development of more interpretable and controllable generative systems, leading to increased adoption and improved workflows in various domains.

*   **Score Justification:**

    GenTune represents a significant contribution to the field of human-AI collaboration in creative workflows. Its innovative design, well-conducted evaluation, and practical implications for environment design warrant a high score. However, the dependency on underlying models and the lack of testing in more domains, holds the paper from a full 10. It's a strong paper that advances the field but could benefit from further exploration of its broader applicability and the broader responsible AI implications of its approach.

### Collaborative Multi-Modal Coding for High-Quality 3D Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15228v1)
- **Authors**: Ziang Cao, Zhaoxi Chen, Liang Pan, Ziwei Liu
- **Abstract**: 3D content inherently encompasses multi-modal characteristics and can be projected into different modalities (e.g., RGB images, RGBD, and point clouds). Each modality exhibits distinct advantages in 3D asset modeling: RGB images contain vivid 3D textures, whereas point clouds define fine-grained 3D geometries. However, most existing 3D-native generative architectures either operate predominantly within single-modality paradigms-thus overlooking the complementary benefits of multi-modality data-or restrict themselves to 3D structures, thereby limiting the scope of available training datasets. To holistically harness multi-modalities for 3D modeling, we present TriMM, the first feed-forward 3D-native generative model that learns from basic multi-modalities (e.g., RGB, RGBD, and point cloud). Specifically, 1) TriMM first introduces collaborative multi-modal coding, which integrates modality-specific features while preserving their unique representational strengths. 2) Furthermore, auxiliary 2D and 3D supervision are introduced to raise the robustness and performance of multi-modal coding. 3) Based on the embedded multi-modal code, TriMM employs a triplane latent diffusion model to generate 3D assets of superior quality, enhancing both the texture and the geometric detail. Extensive experiments on multiple well-known datasets demonstrate that TriMM, by effectively leveraging multi-modality, achieves competitive performance with models trained on large-scale datasets, despite utilizing a small amount of training data. Furthermore, we conduct additional experiments on recent RGB-D datasets, verifying the feasibility of incorporating other multi-modal datasets into 3D generation.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Collaborative Multi-Modal Coding for High-Quality 3D Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TriMM, a novel feed-forward 3D-native generative model designed to leverage multi-modal data (RGB, RGBD, point clouds) for high-quality 3D asset generation. TriMM addresses limitations of existing methods that either focus on single modalities or are restricted to 3D structures, limiting the diversity of training data. The key components of TriMM include: 1) collaborative multi-modal coding to integrate modality-specific features while preserving their strengths, 2) auxiliary 2D and 3D supervision to enhance robustness and performance, and 3) a triplane latent diffusion model for generating superior 3D assets with enhanced texture and geometric detail. The paper demonstrates competitive performance against state-of-the-art methods, even when trained on smaller datasets. TriMM showcases an extensible architecture that can adapt to other multimodal inputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in 3D generative modeling: the scarcity of high-quality 3D training data and the limitations of single-modality approaches.
    *   **Novel multi-modal approach:** The collaborative multi-modal coding is a well-motivated and seemingly effective approach for combining the strengths of different data modalities. The reconstruction-based mechanism guides the model to leverage the best aspects of each modality.
    *   **Extensible Architecture**: TriMM framework exhibits flexibility through modular modality encoders and a shared decoder, which could expand future multimodal data incorporation.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The paper presents extensive quantitative and qualitative results on well-known datasets, demonstrating competitive performance against state-of-the-art methods. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of TriMM. The user study corroborates the quantitative metrics to show human preferences.
    *   **Clear and well-written**: The paper is generally well-structured and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the approach is technically sound and shows promising results, the individual components (encoders, diffusion models, triplane representation, loss functions) are largely based on existing techniques. The primary novelty lies in the specific combination and integration of these components within the proposed framework.
    *   **Limited Data**: In comparison to large-scale generative models that are fed billions of datapoints, TriMM's training data may limit the full potential of its application.
    *   **Dependency on Foundation Models:** The pre-trained foundation models in TriMM's encoder requires additional compute and hardware costs.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper introduces a practical and effective approach to 3D generation that explicitly addresses the data scarcity problem by leveraging multi-modal data.
    *   TriMM's architecture could serve as a foundation for future research on multi-modal 3D generative modeling, enabling the incorporation of new data modalities and techniques.
    *   The results indicate a promising direction for improving the quality and scalability of 3D generative models, with potential applications in virtual reality, robotics, and industrial design.

*   **Rationale for Score:**

    The paper presents a well-executed and effective solution to a relevant problem in 3D generation. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the collaborative multi-modal coding strategy and the overall framework represent a significant contribution to the field. The strong experimental results and ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of TriMM. However, it's important to acknowledge the incremental nature of the novelty and the reliance on existing techniques and model frameworks.

    Therefore, considering the overall contribution, strengths, and weaknesses, the paper warrants a score that reflects a significant contribution while acknowledging the incremental nature of its novelty.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Score**: 7/10

### Pretrained Diffusion Models Are Inherently Skipped-Step Samplers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15233v1)
- **Authors**: Wenju Xu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have been achieving state-of-the-art results across various generation tasks. However, a notable drawback is their sequential generation process, requiring long-sequence step-by-step generation. Existing methods, such as DDIM, attempt to reduce sampling steps by constructing a class of non-Markovian diffusion processes that maintain the same training objective. However, there remains a gap in understanding whether the original diffusion process can achieve the same efficiency without resorting to non-Markovian processes. In this paper, we provide a confirmative answer and introduce skipped-step sampling, a mechanism that bypasses multiple intermediate denoising steps in the iterative generation process, in contrast with the traditional step-by-step refinement of standard diffusion inference. Crucially, we demonstrate that this skipped-step sampling mechanism is derived from the same training objective as the standard diffusion model, indicating that accelerated sampling via skipped-step sampling via a Markovian way is an intrinsic property of pretrained diffusion models. Additionally, we propose an enhanced generation method by integrating our accelerated sampling technique with DDIM. Extensive experiments on popular pretrained diffusion models, including the OpenAI ADM, Stable Diffusion, and Open Sora models, show that our method achieves high-quality generation with significantly reduced sampling steps.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "skipped-step sampling," a technique for accelerating the inference phase of pretrained diffusion models. The core idea is to bypass multiple intermediate denoising steps during the iterative generation process, effectively denoising a noisy input directly from a state further along the standard diffusion trajectory.  The authors theoretically demonstrate that this skipped-step mechanism is intrinsically linked to the training objective of standard diffusion models (like DDPM), implying that pretrained models already possess this capability.  They further enhance their method by integrating it with DDIM to leverage the strengths of both approaches.  Experimental results across image and video generation tasks using various pretrained diffusion models (ADM, Stable Diffusion, Open Sora) show that the method achieves high-quality generation with significantly reduced sampling steps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its theoretical analysis demonstrating that standard diffusion models implicitly support skipped-step sampling.  While existing methods already try to speed up diffusion sampling, the authors provide a formal justification for why this can be done within the original Markovian framework of diffusion models, as opposed to resorting to non-Markovian processes as in DDIM. The integration of skipped-step sampling with DDIM is an incremental advancement, but the core insight about the inherent nature of the original diffusion training is more significant. The idea of directly skipping steps is also not entirely new; however, the connection to the existing objective and the practical application across several large models are useful.

* **Significance:**  The significance of this work comes from several aspects:
    * **Efficiency:**  The potential for significant speedups in diffusion model inference is crucial for practical applications, especially with the increasing adoption of these models in various fields.
    * **Theoretical Understanding:**  The theoretical analysis sheds light on the inner workings of diffusion models, potentially guiding future research on more efficient sampling strategies.
    * **Practical Applicability:** The fact that the method can be applied to existing pretrained models without retraining makes it immediately useful. The results presented showcase improvement in both the quality and efficiency of generative processes.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Solid Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a clear theoretical justification for the skipped-step sampling mechanism.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:** The method is evaluated on a wide range of tasks and pretrained models, demonstrating its generality and effectiveness.  The image quality metrics (IS, FID, Precision, Recall) consistently show improvements.  The inclusion of a recently trending video generation benchmark, Open Sora, is a strong aspect.
    * **Well-written and Clear:** The paper is easy to understand, and the experimental setup is well-described.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Enhancement:** While the core idea is novel, the improvement from integrating with DDIM is perhaps less revolutionary and more of an empirical optimization.
    * **Limited Ablation:** The ablation studies, while present, could be more in-depth. For example, exploring different skipped-step sizes more thoroughly, independent of the integration with DDIM, could have strengthened the analysis.
    * **Comparison with existing methods beyond DDIM:** While comparison with a DDIM baseline is helpful, including comparison to Pseudo-Non-Markovian Sampling (PMLS) and other ODE-based acceleration methods could highlight the specific benefits/drawbacks of the proposed approach.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence future research on diffusion model inference by emphasizing the importance of understanding and exploiting the properties of the original training objective. This might lead to the development of even more efficient sampling techniques that leverage the intrinsic capabilities of these models.  Also, this work makes diffusion more practical by addressing the latency issue.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a theoretically grounded and practically effective technique for accelerating diffusion model inference. The core idea of skipped-step sampling is insightful and well-justified. It builds upon the existing understanding of diffusion models and adds a new perspective by revealing an intrinsic property. Although the integration with DDIM could be viewed as incremental, it contributes to the overall practical value of the work. The significance of the findings lies in its potential to guide future research on diffusion model efficiency and in its immediate applicability to existing pretrained models. The score is slightly reduced due to limited ablation and room for more exploration of comparisons with different acceleration techniques (i.e. ODE-based).

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pathology-Informed Latent Diffusion Model for Anomaly Detection in Lymph Node Metastasis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15236v1)
- **Authors**: Jiamu Wang, Keunho Byeon, Jinsol Song, Anh Nguyen, Sangjeong Ahn, Sung Hak Lee, Jin Tae Kwak
- **Abstract**: Anomaly detection is an emerging approach in digital pathology for its ability to efficiently and effectively utilize data for disease diagnosis. While supervised learning approaches deliver high accuracy, they rely on extensively annotated datasets, suffering from data scarcity in digital pathology. Unsupervised anomaly detection, however, offers a viable alternative by identifying deviations from normal tissue distributions without requiring exhaustive annotations. Recently, denoising diffusion probabilistic models have gained popularity in unsupervised anomaly detection, achieving promising performance in both natural and medical imaging datasets. Building on this, we incorporate a vision-language model with a diffusion model for unsupervised anomaly detection in digital pathology, utilizing histopathology prompts during reconstruction. Our approach employs a set of pathology-related keywords associated with normal tissues to guide the reconstruction process, facilitating the differentiation between normal and abnormal tissues. To evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed method, we conduct experiments on a gastric lymph node dataset from a local hospital and assess its generalization ability under domain shift using a public breast lymph node dataset. The experimental results highlight the potential of the proposed method for unsupervised anomaly detection across various organs in digital pathology. Code: https://github.com/QuIIL/AnoPILaD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AnoPILAD, a novel pathology-informed latent diffusion model for unsupervised anomaly detection in lymph node metastasis.  The method combines a latent diffusion model (LDM) with a vision-language model (VLM) to enhance the identification of anomalies in digital pathology images. The core idea is to guide the LDM's reconstruction process with pathology-specific keywords derived from normal tissue characteristics. A VLM is used to select the most relevant keywords for a given image, creating a weighted prompt.  This guided reconstruction aims to improve the differentiation between normal and abnormal tissues.  The method is evaluated on a local hospital dataset of gastric lymph nodes and a public breast lymph node dataset, demonstrating its ability to generalize and handle domain shifts. The experiments show AnoPILaD outperforms existing unsupervised anomaly detection techniques in terms of AUC, AUPR, DICE and IoU.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty of this work lies in the combination of several existing ideas, but with a specific application and refinement that leads to improved performance.  Specifically:

*   **Diffusion Models for Anomaly Detection:**  Diffusion models have been used in anomaly detection before (as the authors acknowledge, and cite appropriately).
*   **Vision-Language Models:** VLMs have been used for various image analysis tasks.
*   **The Specific Combination:** The authors do propose a novel combination of LDM and a VLM to guide anomaly detection in pathology, using pathology-specific prompts. While not entirely groundbreaking, the application to pathology images and the incorporation of prompts for guiding the reconstruction process is a sensible and effective addition.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper is its potential to improve the accuracy and robustness of unsupervised anomaly detection in digital pathology.  The reliance on expert annotation is a significant bottleneck in the field, and methods like AnoPILAD that can learn from unlabeled data are valuable. Furthermore, the paper addresses an important problem in pathology -- the detection of lymph node metastasis. The improvements in performance compared to existing methods, as demonstrated in the experiments, suggest that the proposed method is a useful advance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges in using supervised learning for anomaly detection in pathology and positions unsupervised learning as a viable solution.
*   **Well-Defined Method:** The approach is clearly explained, and the rationale for each component is justified.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper includes experiments on two different datasets, showing good results for both datasets, proving that the proposed method is better than other anomaly detection models.
*   **Discussion of Results:**  The discussion of the experimental results is thorough, analyzing the strengths and weaknesses of the proposed method.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The novelty of the core idea is somewhat incremental. It's a clever application of existing technologies.
*   **Dataset Size:** While the authors used two different datasets, one of them (C16) has limited size.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of unsupervised anomaly detection in digital pathology. The combination of diffusion models and vision-language models, guided by pathology-specific prompts, is a novel and effective approach. The experimental results demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method over existing techniques. However, the novelty is incremental, and the dataset size might be limiting.
