### Reliable Unlearning Harmful Information in LLMs with Metamorphosis Representation Projection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15449v1)
- **Authors**: Chengcan Wu, Zeming Wei, Huanran Chen, Yinpeng Dong, Meng Sun
- **Abstract**: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance in various domains and tasks, concerns about their safety are becoming increasingly severe. In particular, since models may store unsafe knowledge internally, machine unlearning has emerged as a representative paradigm to ensure model safety. Existing approaches employ various training techniques, such as gradient ascent and negative preference optimization, in attempts to eliminate the influence of undesired data on target models. However, these methods merely suppress the activation of undesired data through parametric training without completely eradicating its informational traces within the model. This fundamental limitation makes it difficult to achieve effective continuous unlearning, rendering these methods vulnerable to relearning attacks. To overcome these challenges, we propose a Metamorphosis Representation Projection (MRP) approach that pioneers the application of irreversible projection properties to machine unlearning. By implementing projective transformations in the hidden state space of specific network layers, our method effectively eliminates harmful information while preserving useful knowledge. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach enables effective continuous unlearning and successfully defends against relearning attacks, achieving state-of-the-art performance in unlearning effectiveness while preserving natural performance. Our code is available in https://github.com/ChengcanWu/MRP.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel machine unlearning approach for Large Language Models (LLMs) called Metamorphosis Representation Projection (MRP). It addresses limitations in existing unlearning methods, specifically their vulnerability to relearning attacks and challenges with continuous unlearning. MRP uses irreversible projection properties in the hidden state space of specific network layers to eliminate harmful information while preserving useful knowledge. It leverages orthogonal projections to remove task-specific information while minimizing impact on other tasks, and uses only a small number of trainable parameters. Experimental results suggest that MRP enables effective continuous unlearning, defends against relearning attacks, and maintains natural performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of representation projection, specifically orthogonal projection, to machine unlearning in LLMs is a relatively novel idea. Projecting representations onto the orthogonal complement of the "retain" data's feature space is a conceptually clean way to eliminate information. The focus on preserving performance in continuous unlearning scenarios is also important and addresses a critical gap in the existing literature. Using orthogonal projections to project the latent representations also minimizes model modifications since the approach only updates the projection matrices.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in the responsible development of LLMs: the ability to reliably remove harmful or unwanted information. The vulnerability of existing unlearning methods to relearning attacks is a major concern, and MRP's defense against these attacks is a crucial step forward. Continuous unlearning is also increasingly important as data access rights change over time. The method is highly effective at both maintaining the utility of models and preventing future access to previously unseen information, indicating the method is able to be used continuously on LLMs without losing any utility.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled Approach:** The method is based on a sound theoretical understanding of representation spaces and orthogonal projections.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of MRP in continuous unlearning and defense against relearning attacks, outperforming existing baselines. It demonstrates significant gains.
    *   **Parameter Efficiency:** MRP achieves state-of-the-art performance with relatively few trainable parameters, making it computationally efficient.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The authors test the method across multiple datasets, model architectures, and unlearning scenarios. The inclusion of experiments evaluating relearning attacks and continuous unlearning is a major strength.
    *   **Robust to Relearning Attacks**: The use of irreversible operations through orthogonal projections ensures the forgotten information is not easily relearned.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Size:** While experiments were conducted on a 7B language model. More recent language models are considerably larger, and may have different emergent properties when utilizing this unlearning method.
    *   **Orthogonality Assumption:** The assumption that retain and unlearn data representations can be effectively separated by orthogonality might not hold in all cases, particularly for more complex or overlapping concepts. While they addressed it's implications, the authors may need to explore more adaptive methods for learning projection subspaces.
    *   **Reliance on Hyperparameter Tuning:** The method has several hyperparameters that need to be tuned for optimal performance, although the authors perform a decent hyperparameter study.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of machine unlearning, particularly in the context of LLMs. The ideas presented could lead to the development of more robust and reliable unlearning techniques.

*   **Justification:** The paper makes a solid contribution to machine unlearning. The performance gains over existing methods, especially in continuous unlearning scenarios and against relearning attacks, are substantial. The theoretical grounding in orthogonal projections is also a significant strength. However, the paper is limited by the scale of the experiments and certain assumptions about data representation.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Dream 7B: Diffusion Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15487v1)
- **Authors**: Jiacheng Ye, Zhihui Xie, Lin Zheng, Jiahui Gao, Zirui Wu, Xin Jiang, Zhenguo Li, Lingpeng Kong
- **Abstract**: We introduce Dream 7B, the most powerful open diffusion large language model to date. Unlike autoregressive (AR) models that generate tokens sequentially, Dream 7B employs discrete diffusion modeling to refine sequences in parallel through iterative denoising. Our model consistently outperforms existing diffusion language models on general, mathematical, and coding tasks. Dream 7B demonstrates superior planning abilities and inference flexibility, including arbitrary-order generation, infilling capabilities, and tunable quality-speed trade-offs. These results are achieved through simple yet effective training techniques, including AR-based LLM initialization and context-adaptive token-level noise rescheduling. We release both Dream-Base and Dream-Instruct to facilitate further research in diffusion-based language modeling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "Dream 7B: Diffusion Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Dream 7B, a 7-billion parameter diffusion language model.  Unlike standard autoregressive (AR) models, Dream 7B leverages discrete diffusion modeling to refine text sequences in parallel through iterative denoising.  The authors claim that Dream 7B outperforms existing diffusion language models on general, mathematical, and coding tasks, demonstrating superior planning abilities and flexible inference. Key techniques contributing to its performance include AR-based LLM initialization and context-adaptive token-level noise rescheduling (CART). The authors release both a base model (Dream-Base) and an instruction-tuned version (Dream-Instruct).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims to present the "most powerful open diffusion large language model to date." While scaling up a diffusion model to 7B parameters is an engineering feat, the core architectural ideas are not entirely novel. The use of discrete diffusion for text generation has been explored previously (Austin et al., 2021a; Hoogeboom et al., 2021).  AR-based LLM initialization and context-adaptive noise scheduling do represent contributions, but their individual novelty is somewhat limited.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance hinges on demonstrating a substantial performance improvement over existing diffusion models and approaching the performance of established AR models. While the authors claim competitive performance with models like Qwen2.5 7B in some areas (or even exceeding AR models in planning) while offering greater inference flexibility (variable timesteps, in-filling), the actual magnitude of improvement requires careful scrutiny. The provided benchmark results do indicate improvement over LLaDA 8B for diffusion models. The planning task numbers (Table 1) strongly support the claim of superior reasoning/planning compared to the equivalently-sized Qwen model. This is a potentially important contribution.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Competitive Performance:**  Demonstrated on several benchmarks, particularly in planning.

    *   **Inference Flexibility:**  Diffusion models offer unique features like controllable quality-speed tradeoffs and arbitrary-order generation, which the authors highlight. These are valuable features not readily available in AR models.

    *   **Engineering Contribution:** Scaling up diffusion models and making them competitive requires overcoming engineering challenges. The proposed training techniques (AR init, CART) appear to be effective.

    *   **Open Release:** Releasing both Dream-Base and Dream-Instruct promotes further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The core idea of diffusion for text is not new; the main contributions are engineering improvements and scaling to a larger model. The architectural additions may be seen as incremental improvements over existing diffusion-based language models.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** It would be beneficial to see ablation studies that more thoroughly evaluate the impact of AR initialization and CART.
    *   **Comparison with SOTA AR models:** While the paper claims competitive performance with Qwen2.5 7B in some areas, it is important to provide a comprehensive performance comparison. Many AR models outperform Qwen2.5 on many of these tasks. More direct comparisons with truly leading AR models would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Lack of novelty with context-adaptive token level noise rescheduling.** Similar concepts have been adopted in other sequence to sequence generation schemes, such as machine translation, with attention mechanism. Although no such citation is given.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the performance claims hold up to wider scrutiny, and if the inference flexibility features prove valuable in practical applications, this paper could encourage more research into diffusion models as an alternative to AR models, particularly in domains where complex reasoning or control over generation is important.

**Score:** 6.5/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents an interesting and useful contribution to the field by scaling up diffusion language models and achieving promising results, particularly in complex reasoning/planning and improved generation compared to other diffusion models. The inference flexibility provided by diffusion models is a definite strength. However, the core ideas are not revolutionary, and the improvements, while significant, are incremental and the lack of novelty within the context-adaptive token level noise rescheduling needs further investigation. The provided code and model releases are a positive aspect. The 6.5 score reflects the engineering contributions and performance improvements without groundbreaking conceptual advancements over what already has been presented in the literature. A higher score would have been warranted if the work presented substantially novel architectural innovations or exceeded the performance of the most powerful autoregressive language models. The current benchmark comparison is limited.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### SynthCoder: A Synthetical Strategy to Tune LLMs for Code Completion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15495v1)
- **Authors**: Dongjun Yu, Xiao Yan, Zhenrui Li, Jipeng Xiao, Haochuan He, Yongda Yu, Hao Zhang, Guoping Rong, Xiaobo Huang
- **Abstract**: Code completion is a prominent application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in software engineering. Due to the near real-time response requirements of this task, base models with small to medium-sized parameters are typically employed, supplemented by various optimization and post-training techniques. However, these optimization methods often have trade-offs, leading to a seesaw effect where performance improvements on certain datasets or metrics are accompanied by degradations on others -- sometimes even falling below the baseline model's performance. This paper proposes SynthCoder, a model that integrates leading industry practices to achieve state-of-the-art performance on the Fill-in-the-Middle (FIM) code completion task. In specific, we first construct a diverse dataset by combining Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) node extraction with heuristics that simulate developer behavior. Then we enrich our training corpus with cross-file contextual information using the BM25 algorithm and call graphs, enhancing the model's ability to perform code completion in both file-level and repository-level scenarios. As the last step, we employ a two-stage training process using the Seed-Coder-8B-Base as the base model. First, we fine-tune the model using Curriculum Learning technology. Following this, we perform alignment using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) with preference pairs generated through Rejection Sampling. Experimental results demonstrate that our final model excels on mainstream repository-level code completion benchmarks, including aiXcoder, ExecRepoBench, CrossCodeEval, and CoLT. Furthermore, our carefully curated training set effectively mitigates the model's tendency to just repeat existing code, a common issue existing in various code completion models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SynthCoder, a methodology for fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) for code completion, specifically targeting the Fill-in-the-Middle (FIM) task. The approach focuses on three key aspects: creating a diverse and realistic training dataset, incorporating cross-file contextual information, and employing a two-stage post-training process consisting of curriculum learning and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) with a rejection sampling-based preference pair generation. The paper demonstrates that SynthCoder achieves state-of-the-art performance on several repository-level code completion benchmarks (aiXcoder, ExecRepoBench, CrossCodeEval, and CoLT).  The work also addresses and mitigates the common problem of code completion models simply repeating existing code from the context.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper's strength lies in its systematic and comprehensive approach to improving code completion models. The combination of diverse data generation, context enrichment, and a two-stage training process is well-reasoned and executed.
    *   **Addressing Real-World Scenarios:** The emphasis on cross-file dependencies and realistic coding habits is crucial for making code completion tools more practical and useful in real-world software development.
    *   **Mitigation of Code Repetition:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in code completion models – the tendency to repeat existing context – and presents a viable solution through its preference alignment training and careful data construction.
    *   **State-of-the-Art Results:** The reported results demonstrate that SynthCoder achieves state-of-the-art performance on established code completion benchmarks, indicating the effectiveness of the proposed methodology.
    *   **Detailed Implementation and Experiments:** The paper provides a clear and detailed account of the data construction process, training methods, and experimental setup, enhancing reproducibility and allowing other researchers to build upon this work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the paper presents a comprehensive approach, the individual components (curriculum learning, DPO, BM25 retrieval, AST-based data generation) are largely based on existing techniques.  The novelty is primarily in the *integration* and careful tuning of these methods.
    *   **Limited Model Focus:** The methodology is primarily validated with a specific base model (Seed-Coder-8B-Base).  While the authors claim it's model-agnostic, extensive validation across different base models would strengthen this claim.
    *   **Evaluation Bias:** The evaluation primarily focuses on existing benchmarks, which, as the authors themselves acknowledge, may not fully capture the nuances of real-world code completion. The limited model variety and dataset makes this more significant.
    *   **Generalizability of specific hyperparameter settings:** Given it's largely an integration of various known techniques, the sensitivity to hyperparameter configuration specific to data sources, dataset sizes is not exhaustively discussed.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the field by providing a practical and effective strategy for improving code completion models.
    *   It highlights the importance of realistic training data and context enrichment for achieving high performance.
    *   The work addresses a common problem in code completion models (code repetition) and offers a practical solution.
    *   The extensive experiments and detailed description of the methodology can serve as a valuable resource for other researchers working in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-executed and comprehensive approach to code completion, achieving state-of-the-art results on standard benchmarks. However, the novelty is somewhat limited by its reliance on existing techniques. While the integration of these techniques is non-trivial and contributes to the overall effectiveness of SynthCoder, the individual components are not groundbreaking. The limitation of focus on the Seed-Coder-8B-Base model and evaluation biases detract from its contribution. It improves existing models for a better fine-tuning outcome which while beneficial, can be considered relatively marginal.

### LLM-Driven Self-Refinement for Embodied Drone Task Planning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15501v1)
- **Authors**: Deyu Zhang, Xicheng Zhang, Jiahao Li, Tingting Long, Xunhua Dai, Yongjian Fu, Jinrui Zhang, Ju Ren, Yaoxue Zhang
- **Abstract**: We introduce SRDrone, a novel system designed for self-refinement task planning in industrial-grade embodied drones. SRDrone incorporates two key technical contributions: First, it employs a continuous state evaluation methodology to robustly and accurately determine task outcomes and provide explanatory feedback. This approach supersedes conventional reliance on single-frame final-state assessment for continuous, dynamic drone operations. Second, SRDrone implements a hierarchical Behavior Tree (BT) modification model. This model integrates multi-level BT plan analysis with a constrained strategy space to enable structured reflective learning from experience. Experimental results demonstrate that SRDrone achieves a 44.87% improvement in Success Rate (SR) over baseline methods. Furthermore, real-world deployment utilizing an experience base optimized through iterative self-refinement attains a 96.25% SR. By embedding adaptive task refinement capabilities within an industrial-grade BT planning framework, SRDrone effectively integrates the general reasoning intelligence of Large Language Models (LLMs) with the stringent physical execution constraints inherent to embodied drones. Code is available at https://github.com/ZXiiiC/SRDrone.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces *SRDrone*, a novel framework for self-refinement in task planning for embodied drones.  SRDrone leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to create plans for drones, but addresses limitations of existing LLM-driven systems related to dependence on human expertise and poor adaptability to new environments. It features two main contributions: (1) a continuous state evaluation methodology (CMSR) that analyzes drone sensor data to provide explanatory feedback for task outcomes, going beyond simple final-state assessment; and (2) a hierarchical Behavior Tree (BT) modification model that analyzes and modifies BTs based on LLM feedback, enabling structured reflective learning. Experimental results in both simulated and real-world settings show SRDrone achieving significantly improved success rates compared to baseline methods.  The code is made available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a well-integrated system addressing a recognized gap in LLM-driven drone task planning. The combination of continuous state evaluation and hierarchical BT modification is particularly noteworthy.  Existing works predominantly focus on either static BT generation or require significant human intervention for adaptation. SRDrone's ability to autonomously refine plans based on execution feedback constitutes a significant step forward. However, the individual components, especially CMSR, are built upon well-established concepts. The real novelty lies in the elegant *integration* of these components in the context of drone task planning and the demonstrated efficacy of the resulting system.
*   **Significance:** The significance of SRDrone lies in its potential to enable more robust and autonomous drone operations in real-world environments. By reducing the dependence on human expertise and improving adaptability, SRDrone addresses key challenges hindering the widespread deployment of drones in various applications such as inspection, delivery, and emergency response. The substantial improvements in success rates reported in the experiments support this claim. The real-world deployment and its success in transferring knowledge from simulation are crucial validation points. However, the paper would be strengthened by a more thorough analysis of failure modes and limitations encountered during real-world testing.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper presents a clear and well-structured approach to self-refinement in drone task planning.
    *   The continuous state evaluation methodology (CMSR) and hierarchical BT modification model are well-defined and justified.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a significant improvement in performance compared to baseline methods.
    *   The real-world deployment validates the operational feasibility of SRDrone.
    *   The code availability promotes reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the integration is novel, the individual components CMSR and hierarchical BT modification are somewhat incremental in terms of technical contribution and could benefit from a stronger connection to the theoretical literature.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the limitations of SRDrone, including potential failure modes and challenges in specific environments.
    *   The comparison to human-guided systems is not as rigorous as the comparison to other automated planning systems, and could be improved by a more detailed analysis of cognitive load and efficiency in human-in-the-loop scenarios.
    *   The paper might overstate the novelty by not adequately emphasizing the reliance on established techniques and models in areas like object detection (YOLOv5) and kNN retrieval.

**Overall Assessment:**

SRDrone represents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM-driven drone task planning. Its ability to autonomously refine plans based on execution feedback is a significant advancement. The paper is well-written, clearly describes the system's components, and provides experimental evidence to support its claims. However, some of the claims of novelty could be tempered, and further discussion of limitations and future research directions would strengthen the paper. The demonstrated real-world applicability elevates its impact.

### Evaluation Guidelines for Empirical Studies in Software Engineering involving LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15503v1)
- **Authors**: Sebastian Baltes, Florian Angermeir, Chetan Arora, Marvin Muñoz Barón, Chunyang Chen, Lukas Böhme, Fabio Calefato, Neil Ernst, Davide Falessi, Brian Fitzgerald, Davide Fucci, Marcos Kalinowski, Stefano Lambiase, Daniel Russo, Mircea Lungu, Lutz Prechelt, Paul Ralph, Christoph Treude, Stefan Wagner
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into software engineering (SE) research and practice, yet their non-determinism, opaque training data, and evolving architectures complicate the reproduction and replication of empirical studies. We present a community effort to scope this space, introducing a taxonomy of LLM-based study types together with eight guidelines for designing and reporting empirical studies involving LLMs. The guidelines present essential (must) criteria as well as desired (should) criteria and target transparency throughout the research process. Our recommendations, contextualized by our study types, are: (1) to declare LLM usage and role; (2) to report model versions, configurations, and fine-tuning; (3) to document tool architectures; (4) to disclose prompts and interaction logs; (5) to use human validation; (6) to employ an open LLM as a baseline; (7) to report suitable baselines, benchmarks, and metrics; and (8) to openly articulate limitations and mitigations. Our goal is to enable reproducibility and replicability despite LLM-specific barriers to open science. We maintain the study types and guidelines online as a living resource for the community to use and shape (llm-guidelines.org).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the growing use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in software engineering research and practice. It highlights the challenges regarding reproducibility and replicability of empirical studies involving LLMs due to their non-determinism, opaque training data, and evolving architectures.  The authors present a taxonomy of LLM-based study types in SE and propose eight guidelines for designing and reporting empirical studies with LLMs. These guidelines cover declaring LLM usage, reporting model details, documenting tool architectures, disclosing prompts and interactions, using human validation, employing open LLMs as baselines, reporting relevant baselines/benchmarks/metrics, and openly articulating limitations. The goal is to improve the transparency, reproducibility, and replicability of LLM-related SE research.  A living online resource is maintained for community feedback.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in consolidating and formalizing best practices for empirical studies involving LLMs specifically within the context of software engineering. While individual recommendations might exist in other contexts (e.g., general AI or data science), this paper provides a specific, tailored approach for the SE community. The taxonomy of study types is a useful contribution for structuring the field.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from the increasing importance of LLMs in SE research and practice. The lack of reproducibility and replicability poses a major threat to the validity of research findings. The guidelines directly address this problem, promoting better science and facilitating more reliable conclusions.  The authors emphasize transparency, which is crucial given the "black box" nature of many LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive coverage of key aspects affecting reproducibility, ranging from model versioning to prompt engineering.
    *   The taxonomy provides a useful framework for categorizing studies and applying appropriate guidelines.
    *   Emphasis on transparency and open science principles.
    *   Practical recommendations with examples, making the guidelines actionable.
    *   The living online resource allows for continuous improvement and community input.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on researchers diligently following the guidelines poses a challenge. Enforcement is absent.
    *   While the guidelines address many challenges, achieving *complete* reproducibility with inherently non-deterministic models remains difficult. More exploration on ways to quantify and report uncertainty could be helpful.
    *   Some guidelines (e.g., using open LLMs as baselines) might be practically challenging for researchers with limited resources. The impact of these practical constraints should be considered.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly improve the quality and trustworthiness of LLM-related SE research.  If widely adopted, it can lead to more reliable results and better-informed decision-making in both research and practice. The guidelines provide a foundation for future work on standardizing evaluation methodologies for LLMs in SE.

*   **Justification:** The guidelines are well-reasoned and address significant concerns in the field. However, challenges in adoption and the inherent limitations of reproducibility due to LLM non-determinism and rapidly evolving models prevent a top score. The impact is highly dependent on community uptake.

### Think in Blocks: Adaptive Reasoning from Direct Response to Deep Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15507v1)
- **Authors**: Yekun Zhu, Guang Chen, Chengjun Mao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) with chains-of-thought have demonstrated strong performance on an increasing range of tasks, particularly those involving complex logical reasoning. However, excessively long chains can lead to overthinking, causing computational waste and slower responses. This raises a question: can LLMs dynamically adjust the length of their reasoning processes based on task complexity? To address this, we propose the Think in Blocks framework, which enables adaptive reasoning-from zero to deep reasoning-by partitioning the reasoning process into a tunable number of blocks. Our main contributions are: (1) Establishing an explicit block-structured paradigm in which the model first predicts an integer reasoning budget-the number of blocks-and then partitions its reasoning accordingly; (2) Training an adaptive model through a three-stage pipeline-Supervised Fine-Tuning, reward-guided Direct Preference Optimization, and Reinforcement Learning-that adjusts its reasoning depth to problem difficulty; (3) Exploiting the explicit block count to dynamically control reasoning depth at inference time, allowing flexible adjustment of chain-of-thought length during deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Think in Blocks: Adaptive Reasoning from Direct Response to Deep Reasoning" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes "Think in Blocks," a framework enabling Large Language Models (LLMs) to adaptively adjust the depth of their reasoning processes based on task complexity. This is achieved by partitioning the reasoning process into a tunable number of discrete blocks. The model is trained to first predict a reasoning budget (number of blocks) based on the problem's perceived difficulty and then generate content for each block. The framework incorporates a three-stage training pipeline: supervised fine-tuning (SFT), reward-guided Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Reinforcement Learning (RL). This allows the model to learn to use fewer blocks for simple questions and more for complex ones.  The explicit block count also provides a control mechanism at inference time, allowing users to trade off accuracy for efficiency by limiting the number of blocks used.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of adaptive reasoning in LLMs is not entirely new. Existing works have explored methods like length penalties, hybrid models that switch between "thinking" and "non-thinking" modes, and DAST-like approaches. However, "Think in Blocks" offers a more granular control over reasoning depth than a simple binary choice (think/no-think).  The partitioning of reasoning into explicit blocks, combined with the prediction of a reasoning budget, seems a novel approach. The use of this explicit budget for both training (via RL and DPO) and inference-time control strengthens the contribution.

*   **Significance:** The overthinking problem in LLMs is a significant concern, leading to wasted computational resources and slow response times.  The ability to dynamically adjust reasoning depth has the potential to improve efficiency and reduce costs, especially for applications with stringent latency requirements. The control mechanism at inference time is a practical feature for deployment.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Granular Control:**  Offers finer-grained control over reasoning depth compared to binary "think/no-think" approaches.
    *   **Training Pipeline:**  The three-stage training pipeline (SFT, DPO, RL) is well-structured and aims to address different aspects of the adaptive reasoning problem.  Using DPO and RL with specific reward functions related to block usage is a good approach to teach the model to be more efficient.
    *   **Inference-Time Control:**  Provides a practical mechanism to trade off accuracy for efficiency by controlling the number of reasoning blocks.
    *   **Experimental Results:** The experiments show that the framework can reduce answer length while maintaining accuracy.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** Introducing blocks and a block count predictor adds complexity to the model. It might require more data and careful hyperparameter tuning.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:**  The creation of the block-structured dataset relies on another LLM (Qwen3-32B) and prompt engineering.  This reliance could introduce biases or limit the generalizability of the approach. The quality of the blocks is critical, and generating coherent blocks may be challenging.
    *   **Practical Challenges:** The paper acknowledges the challenge of aligning predicted and actual block counts. Addressing this requires more robust techniques to enforce consistent formatting.
    *   **DeepMath Dataset Limitation:**  The experiments primarily focus on the DeepMath dataset, which is quite specific. Assessing the performance on a broader range of reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, planning) is needed.

*   **Potential Influence:**  If successful, "Think in Blocks" could influence the design of more efficient and adaptable LLMs. It could also inspire further research into methods for controlling and optimizing the reasoning processes of these models. The idea of using explicit structures (blocks) for reasoning could be applicable to other tasks beyond mathematical reasoning. The influence will be tied to the performance on general reasoning benchmarks though.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the core idea of adaptive reasoning isn't entirely new, the specific implementation using explicit blocks and a well-defined training pipeline shows some novelty. The potential significance lies in addressing the overthinking problem and providing practical control mechanisms for deployment. However, the challenges related to dataset dependency, practical implementation issues, and limited benchmark used temper the enthusiasm.

### Super-additive Cooperation in Language Model Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15510v1)
- **Authors**: Filippo Tonini, Lukas Galke
- **Abstract**: With the prospect of autonomous artificial intelligence (AI) agents, studying their tendency for cooperative behavior becomes an increasingly relevant topic. This study is inspired by the super-additive cooperation theory, where the combined effects of repeated interactions and inter-group rivalry have been argued to be the cause for cooperative tendencies found in humans. We devised a virtual tournament where language model agents, grouped into teams, face each other in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. By simulating both internal team dynamics and external competition, we discovered that this blend substantially boosts both overall and initial, one-shot cooperation levels (the tendency to cooperate in one-off interactions). This research provides a novel framework for large language models to strategize and act in complex social scenarios and offers evidence for how intergroup competition can, counter-intuitively, result in more cooperative behavior. These insights are crucial for designing future multi-agent AI systems that can effectively work together and better align with human values. Source code is available at https://github.com/pippot/Superadditive-cooperation-LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the emergence of cooperative behavior in Language Model (LLM) agents within a simulated Iterated Prisoner's Dilemma (IPD) tournament.  Inspired by the "super-additive cooperation" theory, the study investigates how combining repeated interactions within groups (RI) and competition between groups (GC) influences cooperation rates.  LLM agents (Qwen3 14b, Phi4 reasoning, and Cogito 14b) are placed in three tournament structures: RI only, GC only, and a combined SA (super-additive) structure. A self-reflection prompting framework is used to enable strategic planning and adaptation by the agents. The study finds that the super-additive condition often leads to higher cooperation and one-shot cooperation rates, although this effect depends on the specific LLM used and its capacity to understand the game dynamics. Meta-prompting is employed to assess the agents' understanding of the game.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a novel investigation into super-additive cooperation within LLM agents by integrating repeated interactions within groups and competition between groups. While previous work has explored cooperation in LLMs using IPD, the specific framing and experimental design drawing from the super-additive cooperation theory offer a fresh perspective. The systematic comparison across different tournament structures (RI, GC, SA) is a strength.

*   **Significance:** The findings contribute to the growing understanding of how LLM agents behave in social contexts, which is crucial for developing safe and aligned AI systems. The identification of conditions that promote cooperation is valuable. The study also highlights the importance of model-specific characteristics and comprehension levels in shaping cooperation dynamics. The paper directly addresses a growing concern within the field of AI safety concerning the potential for adversarial or uncooperative behavior amongst agents in competitive environments, suggesting ways of mitigating these behaviors.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear experimental design with well-defined conditions.
    *   Use of multiple LLMs to compare behavior.
    *   Inclusion of a self-reflection prompting framework.
    *   Analysis of both cooperation rate and one-shot cooperation.
    *   Use of meta prompting to measure understanding.
    *  Includes and adapts a well-established cooperation theory from social sciences.
    * Addresses a critical and important real world problem within AI research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study is limited to three relatively lightweight, open-source LLMs. This raises concerns about generalizability to more powerful, proprietary models. The use of different training data, architecture, or pre-training methods among those models could mean that results might not hold true when scaling.
    *   The number of agents and rounds in the tournament is limited by computational constraints. It is possible that more complex strategic behaviors would emerge with larger simulations.
    *   The IPD is a simplified model of social interaction. The findings may not fully translate to more complex, real-world scenarios. The lack of complexity may hinder the validity of their argument.
    *   While the prompts were designed to be neutral, subtle biases in the prompting strategy could influence the LLM's behavior.
    *  Includes assumptions in implementation details: The number of matches and team structure choices could result in an unrepresentative sample.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM agent behavior, particularly in the context of multi-agent systems and AI safety. The super-additive cooperation framework provides a useful lens for analyzing and promoting cooperation. The study also highlights the need for further research into model-specific characteristics and comprehension levels. The proposed interaction structure can be beneficial for designers, potentially avoiding adversarial behavior among AI agents.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper clearly presents its methodology, results, and limitations. The discussion section provides a thoughtful interpretation of the findings and their implications. However, the limitations related to model scale, simulation complexity, and prompt sensitivity are significant and warrant a more critical discussion. Overall, the paper's theoretical framework provides a good foundation for follow-up research regarding large language models interacting in tournament settings, and presents interesting results.
